#5132. Bitcoin ETFs flip negative for 2026 after $1.28 billion outflow week, worst since June

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

The 12 U.S.-listed spot bitcoin funds have now lost $914.1 million for the year, while ether, Solana and Hyperliquid ETFs all extended weekly outflow streaks in a brutal turn for crypto ETFs.

U.S. spot bitcoin ETFs logged $1.28 billion in net outflows last week, their worst week since late June, per The Block's analysis of SoSoValue data. It was the funds' second straight negative week and made the top ten largest weekly outflows since they launched in January 2024 at #7.

The funds saw outflows every day except Wednesday last week, per the data. They lost $398.6 million on Monday, $115.6 million on Tuesday, $430.2 million on Thursday and $411.9 million on Friday, with Wednesday's relatively modest $74.6 million inflow marking the only positive day.

The most recent worse week ended June 26, when the funds shed $1.79 billion in what The Block reported at the time was their second-worst week on record. Bitcoin ETFs lost $202.6 million the week before last, including a $643.3 million Friday outflow that was the largest single-day exit in four months after the record Binance hack.

BlackRock's IBIT accounted for $403 million of Friday's loss, or about 88% of the day's gross outflows. Bitwise's BITB and Ark & 21Shares' ARKB each lost about $20 million, and Grayscale's GBTC shed $11.1 million. Fidelity's FBTC took in $42.8 million on Friday, after leading Thursday's outflows with $199.9 million, as The Block reported.

The week's outflows erased bitcoin ETFs' gains for the year. The funds entered the week with about $368 million in 2026 net inflows, after turning positive for the year in late September. Monday's outflows pushed that figure below zero, and the funds closed Friday at about $914.1 million in net outflows year to date.

The total value of bitcoin held across the 12 funds dropped 2.3% to $95.9 billion, equal to about 6.3% of the cryptocurrency's market capitalization. IBIT held $60.3 billion in net assets against $65.1 billion in cumulative inflows.

The outflows came amid a crypto and equities selloff after Tuesday's midterm elections. Bitcoin fell 5.6% on Thursday alone, and analysts linked the slide to the Democratic sweep of Congress and doubts over AI spending, The Block reported Friday. Analysts say Texas's blue wave in particular could result in slowed development in the state replete with bitcoin mining facilities and data centers.

Spot ether ETFs lost $365.3 million last week, per SoSoValue data, their sixth straight week of net outflows. The run has drained about $2.02 billion from the funds since late September. Ether ETFs have twice posted eight-week losing streaks, most recently when they matched that record in early July.

Like the bitcoin funds, ether ETFs had only one positive trading day last week, also on Wednesday, when they gained $53 million. Monday's $139.8 million outflow was the week's largest, followed by $100 million on Thursday and $93.2 million on Friday. BlackRock's ETHA accounted for $90.8 million of Friday's total, about 96% of the gross losses, mirroring the firm's bitcoin fund.

The products are now down about $405.6 million for 2026. Net assets fell 2.9% over the week to $12.84 billion, against $11.92 billion in cumulative net inflows.

Spot Solana ETFs lost $26.3 million last week, per SoSoValue, nearly all of it on Friday. It was their fifth straight negative week, the longest streak since the funds launched in October 2025, and brought five-week outflows to $165.4 million. The streak began the week the funds ended a record 14-week inflow run.

Bitwise's BSOL led Friday's Solana outflows with $14.4 million, followed by Grayscale's GSOL at $5.4 million. The funds held $1.31 billion in net assets as of Friday, down from $1.40 billion a week earlier, though they remain about $676.2 million positive for the year.

The three publicly traded Hyperliquid ETFs shed $15.6 million, also extending their outflow streak to five weeks, the longest since they began trading in May. The funds have lost $58.4 million over that stretch, and their net assets fell about 17% on the week to $374.1 million. Friday's $8 million outflow was led by Bitwise's BHYP.

Bitcoin traded near $75,880 on Saturday morning, while ether changed hands around $2,244, SOL near $89.84 and HYPE around $77.79, according to The Block's price pages.

#5129. Ex-FBI agent pleads guilty to stealing $2.47 million in crypto from wallets tied to 35 investigations

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Court filings show Patrick Yaroch drained at least 79 wallets, including some belonging to crime victims, and planned to use the money to buy a vineyard in Portugal.

Former FBI agent Patrick Steven Yaroch admitted on Thursday to stealing about $2.47 million in cryptocurrency from wallets tied to FBI investigations, according to the U.S. Attorney's Office for the Eastern District of Virginia.

Yaroch, 37, of Ashburn, Virginia, pleaded guilty to one count each of wire fraud and money laundering related to the thefts. Each count carries up to 20 years in prison, and sentencing is set for March 4, 2027.

Beginning no later than December 2024, Yaroch searched FBI databases, including a classified one, for crypto credentials, according to the statement of facts he signed. He used the information he found to make about 100 transfers out of at least 79 wallets connected to 35 FBI investigations.

Most of the wallets belonged to people under FBI investigation, and some were controlled by two adversarial nation states the filing does not name. NBC News reported in August, citing two sources, that the adversarial nation in the original complaint was Russia. Yaroch also made at least 10 transfers from wallets belonging to crime victims in other FBI cases, and at least one from a wallet the FBI had funded for an operation.

The filing calls Yaroch "indiscriminate," with individual thefts ranging from 81 cents to more than $580,000. After each theft, he kept checking FBI systems to make sure no one had noticed.

Yaroch joined the FBI's Boston Division in 2017, working on a squad focused on one of the two nation states. From February 2025 until July 31, 2026, he was a supervisory special agent in the Counterintelligence and Espionage Division at FBI headquarters.

Yaroch had racked up significant debts before the scheme began: he had taken out more than $100,000 in loans in 2024 and owed more than $45,000 on credit cards, according to the filing.

Between Nov. 25, 2025, and Jan. 13, 2026, Yaroch moved about $93,082 to a TD Bank account and used it to pay down debts, including about $30,176 on credit cards and $20,000 toward a Lexus, per the filing. Yaroch initially said he stole the crypto out of frustration, but prosecutors wrote in an August court filing that he "should not be viewed as a selfless Robin Hoodesque figure."

On June 4, Yaroch asked ChatGPT what it would do with about $1 million if it wanted to leave the U.S. and live in a European Union country. The chatbot's answer cited his wife and child, "the desire for a 2-5 hectare estate, interest in age-worthy red wine," and named Portugal "my top choice for your specific situation," according to the filing.

On June 15, Yaroch gave two Portuguese lawyers power of attorney to register him with the country's tax authority. He also arranged a September trip to Portugal tied to his planned vineyard purchase. Prosecutors also said in the August filing that Yaroch had failed to report a 10-day trip in May, when he flew to Germany and came home from Portugal.

The prosecutors asked to keep him jailed before trial, citing about $160,000 he still held on Kraken and possible Espionage Act charges that were never filed. U.S. District Judge Anthony Trenga instead released him on a $50,000 bond with location monitoring, under conditions that bar him from using video game consoles.

Yaroch initially agreed to give agents a piece of paper that appeared to contain his seed phrase, according to the August filing. However, he later asked for it back after agents told him he had the right to stay silent and that what he said could be used against him in a criminal case.

When agents found the wallet he had pointed them to, $1.02 million had already moved out. Yaroch then admitted the funds were on the lending platform Suilend and helped them reinstall a wallet app he had deleted from his phone.

Under the plea agreement, Yaroch must pay at least $2.4 million in restitution and faces a forfeiture judgment of at least $2.4 million. He also agreed to forfeit 17,990.67 USDC and about 1.38 million SUI seized on July 31, worth roughly $1.2 million at current prices, plus the contents of his TD Bank, Charles Schwab, Kraken and BloFin accounts.

Yaroch acknowledged that the full proceeds of the scheme "are not available." He also agreed to be interviewed by Justice Department and FBI officials about his crimes.

If there's eventually a Netflix documentary about the case, Yaroch won't be profiting: any money he makes from books, films, articles or interviews about his FBI work or his case will go to the U.S. government. He must also submit any such work to the FBI for review before publication.

The Block reported in August that Yaroch had been fired on July 31 and charged with transporting and receiving stolen money. At the time, the Justice Department's Office of the Inspector General said he had moved about $1 million in roughly 10 transfers and had reported himself to two Justice Department employees on July 29.

Earlier this year, the FBI arrested John Daghita in Saint Martin over the alleged theft of more than $46 million in crypto from the U.S. Marshals Service. Onchain investigator ZachXBT alleged that Daghita is the son of an executive at a firm contracted to help the agency manage seized crypto.

A federal grand jury in Alexandria indicted Daghita on 15 counts in March, and a French appeals court in Guadeloupe approved his extradition to the U.S. in May, according to local broadcaster RCI. His arraignment is set for Nov. 12 in Alexandria, according to his court docket.

#5128. Strategy (MSTR) Adds 1,036 Bitcoin for $81.9 Million as Holdings Slip Below Cost

Strategy bought 1,036 bitcoin last week for $81.9 million at an average of $79,012, lifting its stack to 850,364 BTC.

Strategy bought another 1,036 bitcoin last week. The Saylor-led firm spent $81.9 million at an average of $79,012 per coin between November 2 and November 8, a Monday 8-K filing shows, lifting total holdings to 850,364 BTC bought for $64.16 billion at an average of $75,456.

The coins were funded with net proceeds from MSTR common stock sales. Strategy sold about 2.05 million shares for $283.8 million, put $81.9 million into bitcoin, spent $188.3 million buying back its STRC preferred and parked $13.6 million in USD cash. No preferred stock was sold under the ATM during the week.

That buyback retired about 1.89 million STRC shares. Strategy still has $560.3 million left under its preferred repurchase program and $1.0 billion under its MSTR buyback authorization, with about $18.3 billion of MSTR still available under the ATM.

Executive Chairman Michael Saylor posted the update on X: “Strategy has acquired 1,036 $BTC and repurchased $188M of $STRC. As of 11/8/26, we hold 850,364 BTC and $4.9B of USD Assets.”

On Sunday he shared his usual “dots” tracker chart with the line “Connecting the dots,” a tease that has often preceded Monday buy filings. The chart landed after a weekend slide that pushed bitcoin toward $74,000, its weakest level since August.

The filing lands as the spot bitcoin price sits below Strategy’s average cost. At the time of writing it trades near $74,800, about 0.9% under the $75,456 basis, leaving the stack roughly $550 million underwater on paper. The prior week’s average cost was $75,451, so the new purchase nudged the basis up by only $5 even though those 1,036 coins were bought at nearly $79,000.

Strategy’s ledger shows that was not the first time the firm has run a thin cushion this year. Between June 30 and August 10 it sold bitcoin in four consecutive filings — 1,363, then 2,225, then 1,638, then 1,690 coins — to fund preferred dividends and STRC buybacks before flipping back to accumulation.

Those sales cut the stack to 840,447 BTC at their low. Strategy has since rebuilt past that mark and now holds more than 4% of bitcoin’s 21 million supply cap. The pace of buying has slowed since the summer sales. Strategy added 811 bitcoin the week before this one and skipped buys entirely the week of October 19 through 25 while spending about $205 million on STRC.

STRC is designed to trade near its $100 par. Strategy has spent more than $2 billion retiring the preferred this year as the shares oscillated around that level, and shareholders recently approved daily dividends on STRC, STRF, STRK and STRD. Daily accrual for STRC began November 1.

This week’s mix put more ATM cash into preferred buybacks than into bitcoin. The $188.3 million STRC repurchase was more than twice the $81.9 million bitcoin spend, a pattern that has become familiar since Strategy built its USD Reserve and USD Cash buckets over the summer. Last week’s 811-coin purchase was also paired with a larger STRC buyback, then funded from USD cash rather than fresh stock sales.

As of November 8, the USD Reserve stood at $4.77 billion and USD Cash at $146.2 million. Strategy used $21.1 million of the reserve during the week to pay preferred dividends. The SEC 8-K is the primary disclosure; Strategy also posts the same figures on its public dashboard.

MSTR closed Friday at $128.93 after a rough stretch that included a 9% weekly drop into the October 30 close, and the stock was indicated lower in premarket trading Monday. At the time of writing, bitcoin is near $74,800, Bitcoin Magazine Pro data shows. The test now is whether Strategy keeps buying through an underwater stack or leans harder on preferred buybacks while the ATM stays open.

#5127. US Treasury cuts off MEXC over alleged $16B in illicit transactions

The US Treasury bureau FinCEN on Friday ordered US financial institutions to stop processing transfers involving MEXC Global.

The US Department of Treasury on Friday issued an order to cut cryptocurrency exchange MEXC Global off from the US’ financial system after accusing it of facilitating at least $16 billion in transactions linked to sanctions evasion, cybercrime and other illicit activity.

The Treasury’s Financial Crimes Enforcement Network (FinCEN) asserted that MEXC handled transactions involving sanctioned groups in Iran, Russia and North Korea between May 2021 and May 2026. This included around $1.5 billion in transactions linked to Russian sanctions evasion, according to the official press release.

The order effectively bans US financial institutions from processing money transfers involving MEXC and will take effect 30 days after it is published in the Federal Register.

“Financial institutions cannot have it both ways: Either you support the Iranian regime, or you have access to the U.S. financial system,” said Secretary of the Treasury Scott Bessent. “Treasury will continue to sever the Iranian regime’s economic lifelines and thwart its desperate attempts to circumvent U.S. sanctions using digital assets.”

The action is part of Operation Economic Outcast, a US Treasury campaign launched in August with aims to cut off financial entities that help Iran evade sanctions.

According to DefiLlama, the exchange held about $4.48 billion in total assets as of Friday. Cointelegraph contacted MEXC for comment but had not received a response by publication.

#5126. Bitcoin ETFs Bleed $430 Million as Tech Rout Tests Wall Street Buyers

Bitcoin ETFs Bleed $430 Million as Tech Rout Tests Wall Street Buyers

U.S. spot Bitcoin exchange-traded funds lost $430.2 million to net withdrawals Thursday as a technology-stock selloff spread to crypto, with Fidelity and BlackRock leading the exits.

Fidelity's Wise Origin Bitcoin Fund (FBTC) posted $199.9 million in net outflows and BlackRock's iShares Bitcoin Trust (IBIT) shed $105 million, according to Farside Investors. Together they accounted for about 71% of the day's withdrawals.

Grayscale's GBTC lost another $49.9 million, while Franklin Templeton's EZBC shed $32.9 million. No fund in Farside's table recorded positive net flows Thursday, although several reported no net movement.

The latest withdrawals brought this week's net outflow to $869.8 million, following a $643.2 million outflow last Friday. Wednesday's modest $74.6 million inflow offered a brief reprieve before Thursday's reversal.

Fidelity's swing was particularly sharp: FBTC attracted $236.7 million Wednesday, then surrendered nearly $200 million the next session. BlackRock, meanwhile, lost $201.2 million even on Wednesday's positive day, extending a five-session withdrawal streak since October 30 to roughly $1.04 billion.

ETF flows have often been treated as a gauge of demand from investors accessing Bitcoin through regular brokerage accounts. The latest figures show that access works both ways: the same familiar wrapper that makes buying easy also makes exiting straightforward.

Because ETFs trade on stock exchanges, investors can adjust their Bitcoin exposure without transferring coins to a crypto wallet. That convenience can also make the funds an easy place to cut risk during an equity selloff. Daily net flows capture shares created or redeemed at the fund level, not every trade between investors.

Thursday's redemptions arrived as the Nasdaq Composite plunged 5.5% and the S&P 500 fell about 4.4%. Chipmakers and data-center stocks led the decline as investors questioned the pace of artificial-intelligence spending and potential restrictions after the U.S. midterm elections.

Bitcoin also surrendered its post-election rebound, falling back toward $76,000 after trading above $80,000 Wednesday. Short liquidations had helped power that rally, making the subsequent reversal more painful for leveraged traders.

The ETF figures add another piece to that picture. Net flows measure creations minus redemptions of ETF shares; they cannot tell whether holders were raising cash to cover losses elsewhere, trimming Bitcoin exposure, or rebalancing portfolios.

#5119. Paraguay Protests Over Bank Lockout Intensify as Impeachment Vote Nears

Paraguay Protests Over Bank Lockout Intensify as Impeachment Vote Nears

Thousands of Paraguayans marched through Asunción this week demanding their money back after Ueno Bank, a digital lender, locked about 3 million customers out of their accounts, according.

That is about 60% of Paraguay’s adults. The bank also held $800 million to $850 million in public money, including state pension and health funds, according to the AP, so the fallout reaches the pension system.

Anger boiled over Thursday night outside Ueno’s headquarters in Asunción, where savers burned debit and credit cards. Paraguayo “Payo” Cubas, a former presidential candidate, tried to join and was booed, shoved, and forced out.

Protesters shouted “divisor” and “oportunista,” meaning divider and opportunist, accusing him of trying to profit politically from public rage, according to the same report.

Diario Hoy reported that protesters also blamed him for the Senate’s current makeup, saying Cubas put Zenaida Delgado and Javier Vera on his party’s legislative ticket, the slate of candidates voters choose from, and that both joined the government’s side soon after taking office.

He then said he would face impeachment—a trial in Congress to remove a president—and defend himself.

The lower house had already voted 78-0 to open impeachment, and on Friday the Senate rejected his request for 72 extra hours to prepare his defense. The Senate planned a removal vote for Friday night, ABC Color reported, with Vice President Pedro Alliana next in line.

Horacio Cartes, the Colorado Party leader and Peña’s former political mentor, withdrew his support and backed the impeachment. “We will not be on the side of the plunder of public, private and retirees’ funds,” Cartes said.

Prosecutors also opened a criminal investigation into Peña’s possible responsibility for the bank’s irregularities, which he denies.

Police used water cannons and tear gas on marchers near Congress, and a new law requires most deposits to be returned within five business days, though protesters have not been placated.

The central bank says its deposit guarantee covers up to 228.3 million guaraníes per person, about $36,000, but as Decrypt previously reported, the first payouts will go only to accounts with up to 10 million guaraníes (around $1,600) and no debts to Ueno.

Bitcoiners, of course, rushed to use Paraguay’s banking crisis as an example on the benefits of decentralization and self custody, and why the “Bitcoin fixes this” motto is still relevant in today’s financial system. “Bitcoin adoption at a nation-state level can mitigate these types of situations,” said JAN3 founder Samson Mow. “If BTC is available to the people as a parallel rail, then systemic banking failures have far less impact.”

#5116. Vitalik Buterin says ETH should be valued as a store of value, not a fee-burning token

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

The Ethereum co-founder said the case for valuing ETH as a fee-burning token "never was as strong as it seemed" and that maximizing fees would force Ethereum to operate like a major corporation.

Ethereum co-founder Vitalik Buterin said he believes ether Ethereum (ETH) should be treated primarily as a store of value, arguing that the case for valuing it as a fee-burning token was weaker than it appeared during the last market cycle.

In an X thread on Sunday morning, Buterin said "ETH as store of value" makes "much more sense to focus on" than "ETH as fee-burning token." He said EIP-1559, the 2021 upgrade that burns part of every transaction fee, "is fine and good," and the network should keep the mechanism and its burn, even if the burn only partially offsets the issuance of new ETH. "But we should not feel sad if fee burn is < 10% of breakeven indefinitely; the value of ETH comes from elsewhere," he wrote.

The fee burn was the basis of the "ultrasound money" thesis that gained traction among ETH holders during the 2021 bull market. Buterin, in his thread, laid out the valuation model behind fee-burning tokens, in which a token's market cap would equal the annual burn divided by a discount rate. Under that model, a token burning $1 billion a year at a 4% discount rate would have a natural valuation floor of $25 billion, he wrote.

"But here's the problem: this was never a true floor," Buterin wrote. He said most of the ETH burned in 2021 through 2023 came from "crypto-internal use cases" whose revenue would have fallen alongside ETH's price, citing the network "randomly [going] crazy" for a "digital monkey sale" that burned $180 million in fees in a single day. "The floor argument only works if the backing of the revenue stream is external," he wrote.

(Actually, the sale Buterin referenced was for digital land in the Otherside metaverse from Yuga Labs, the creators of the Bored Ape Yacht Club NFT collection. The sale saw users paying up to $10,000 in transaction fees for Otherdeed NFTs that currently sell for around $150.)

Buterin said Ethereum now has a "better claim to this external backing" because of the growing market for tokenized real-world assets. But as a "normal stable mature business," that activity is unlikely to produce the same fee spikes, he added.

The amount of ETH burned each day has fallen sharply since then, according to The Block's data. Ethereum burned about 1.48 million ETH in 2022, worth roughly $3.6 billion, compared with about 19,200 ETH, or $43 million, so far this year.

Buterin said that justifying ETH's market cap, which he put at about $270 billion, through fee burn alone would make Ethereum comparable to a company like L'Oreal. Ether's market cap stood at $273.3 billion on Sunday morning, according to The Block's ETH price page.

Companies of that size spend heavily to keep their positions, Buterin wrote. Citing an AI estimate that L’Oreal spends about 0.58% of its market cap on research and development each year, he said matching that rate would “bankrupt the [Ethereum Foundation] almost immediately,” so it would instead require in-protocol funding of about 700,000 ETH a year, cutting validator revenue by two-thirds.

What Buterin left unstated: the level of funding received by the Ethereum Foundation (EF) is already somewhat controversial in the crypto community.

The EF's ETH sales have regularly drawn criticism from holders, including after it sold 20,000 ETH worth about $47 million to Bitmine over two weeks in April and May. Buterin said in May that he regularly hears from community members who feel the EF's actions don't match its public commitments.

The EF began staking part of its treasury earlier this year to generate yield, and in March it published a mandate describing itself as "one of many stewards" of the network. Amid a wave of senior staff departures in May, Buterin said the foundation would become "a smaller ship" that sells less ETH and focuses on work that "would not happen otherwise."

Buterin said growth in assets issued natively on Ethereum's base layer, including real-world assets, is "highly synergistic" with the store-of-value case because it expands what holders can do with ETH, such as using it as collateral.

Buterin views Ethereum as "really not just a blockchain anymore," but rather "a hybrid architecture that combines together blockchains and modern cryptography." In September, he mapped out Ethereum's roadmap through 2030 and said the upcoming Hegota upgrade is likely to be the network's last "normal" fork.

Buterin's post garnered support from other influential figures in crypto. Solana co-founder Anatoly Yakovenko said Buterin "is absolutely right that you can't focus on fees as the product." Attorney Gabriel Shapiro of MetaLeX called the thread "a 'nature is healing' moment," while Moonrock Capital founder Simon Dedic argued that ETH as a store of value would be "the biggest bull case for Ethereum. And at the same time the easiest story to sell to institutional capital."

The thread comes about five weeks after the co-authors of controversial proposal EIP-8363 withdrew it from consideration for the network's forthcoming Hegota upgrade, which will follow the behind-schedule Glamsterdam hard fork. The proposal, which would have burned a rising share of validator rewards as more ETH is staked, cited the risk a high staking ratio poses to "ETH's role as money" and drew opposition from SharpLink CEO Joseph Chalom and Aave founder Stani Kulechov. Its authors plan to hold a separate issuance process that runs through EthCC in April.

ETH traded at $2,236 as of 9:23 a.m. ET, down 0.3% on the day, according to The Block's price page.

Suspicious prediction market bets were placed shortly before two recent New York Times/Siena political polls were released to the public, according to a report.

#5113. Coinbase Wins Full Federal Trust Charter From OCC

Coinbase has won a full national trust bank charter from the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency, upgrading the conditional approval it received in April and putting its institutional custody business under direct federal supervision, the exchange said Monday.

The OCC approved the application for Coinbase National Trust Company last Friday, the company said in a blog post. OCC licensing records, as reported, show the chartering process was completed Nov. 6.

Coinbase applied in October 2025 and received preliminary approval on April 2. In the intervening months it had to finish its organizational and operational preparations before the OCC would let the trust bank open.

“This charter allows us to bring clear and consistent federal uniformity to our industry-leading custody capabilities,” Coinbase wrote, adding that it strengthens the regulatory framework under which it serves customers. The company said it has served as a custodian for more than 14 years.

A national trust bank can hold assets for clients as a fiduciary, and Coinbase’s focus is custody for institutional customers. It is not a commercial bank. It cannot take ordinary retail deposits or make loans, and the charter does not authorize it to issue stablecoins.

Staking, prime trading and financing will keep running through Coinbase affiliates, not the trust itself. Under the OCC’s April decision, the trust bank acts as an intermediary for those services and does not perform them directly.

The decision also sets conditions. The trust bank must hold at least $60 million in Tier 1 capital and enough liquid assets to cover 180 days of operating expenses during its first three years.

The license does not automatically connect Coinbase to Federal Reserve payment systems either. That requires a separate application under the Fed’s account access guidelines.

Coinbase already runs custody through Coinbase Custody Trust Company, a limited-purpose trust regulated by the New York State Department of Financial Services. The company plans to move that business to the national entity over three years.

Coinbase is the latest crypto firm to complete a federal trust charter. Ripple received final approval on Oct. 26, and Circle won final OCC approval in July, a decision that put USDC reserves on a path toward federal supervision.

The OCC has been courting the sector. In August, Comptroller Jonathan V. Gould said “America and the OCC are once again open for business,” as the regulator touted its charter push. Ripple, Circle, Crypto.com and Paxos had all received conditional approval by then. Block applied for its own banking charter in September.

The approval did not come without resistance. The Independent Community Bankers of America urged the OCC in December to reject Coinbase’s application, arguing the exchange has “demonstrably flawed risk and control functions.” The American Bankers Association asked the regulator in February to slow its review of crypto charter requests.

Underneath those objections is a turf war over stablecoins. Coinbase wants to pay customers rewards for holding the tokens, and banks say that could erode their deposit base.

Coinbase (COIN) did not disclose any financial impact from the charter in its announcement, which framed the approval as a regulatory milestone.

The timing is notable. The approval arrives days after Democrats won both chambers of Congress, and a charter issued by an executive-branch regulator sits outside any legislative fight, at least for now. Vice Chairman Ryan VanGrack said the company has spent years “asking for clear rules and rigorous oversight.” The company also said the approval is “another major step” in integrating digital assets into the American financial system.

The next test is whether Coinbase moves its existing custody clients over on schedule, and whether the community bankers who opposed the charter take their objections to court or to Congress.

#5112. Why a Hyperliquid Treasury Firm Wants to Become a US Futures Broker

Why a Hyperliquid Treasury Firm Wants to Become a US Futures Broker

Hyperliquid Strategies built its business around holding HYPE. Now it wants to help Americans trade on Hyperliquid, too.

The Nasdaq-listed treasury firm, which trades as PURR and is chaired by former Barclays CEO Bob Diamond, disclosed in its quarterly results Thursday that a subsidiary has applied to become a futures commission merchant, or FCM.

That’s the type of CFTC-registered broker that holds customer funds and handles futures orders. If approved, the unit would carry U.S. client accounts for permissioned markets built on Hyperliquid, letting Americans trade perpetual futures within a regulated framework.

Perpetual futures are derivatives with no expiration date that let traders take leveraged positions. Hyperliquid, a major onchain venue for them, still blocks U.S. users.

Its HIP-3 framework lets outside firms launch their own perpetual markets on Hyperliquid’s order book after staking HYPE. A new permissioned version, called HIP-3*, would let a regulated deployer run markets restricted to approved traders, with Hyperliquid acting as neutral infrastructure.

“We deployed $802 million this quarter and filed an application to become a futures commission merchant,” CEO David Schamis said. “Those two moves are the same bet.”

The firm held about 38.5 million HYPE at the end of September, worth roughly $3.5 billion, and more U.S. trading routed through Hyperliquid would likely mean more fees for the protocol, which uses fee revenue to buy back HYPE. An FCM would also give Hyperliquid Strategies a business of its own beyond holding tokens.

During the quarter, the firm spent $785 million buying 9.1 million HYPE and $17 million repurchasing 2.7 million of its own shares, funded largely by $911 million raised through stock sales. It reported $620.6 million in net income, driven almost entirely by $803 million in unrealized gains as HYPE rose from about $65 to $91.

It won’t be alone. Kraken parent Payward has said it plans to deploy HIP-3* markets through its CFTC-regulated Bitnomial exchange, with NinjaTrader Clearing serving as the FCM.

And last week, NYSE owner Intercontinental Exchange agreed to team up with Trade[XYZ] to bring Hyperliquid-based perpetual futures to the U.S., sending HYPE up 13%. Trade[XYZ] already runs markets on Hyperliquid, including perpetual futures tied to the S&P 500.

Regulators must sign off on both the FCM registration and the permissioned HIP-3 markets it would serve, and the company has given no timeline.

#5110. Web3Auth says it frontran hackers by moving $5.6 million out of exposed Keplr wallets

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Consensys' Web3Auth team said it used a flaw that exposed some Keplr Wallet users' private keys to move their funds to safe addresses. Its 12 recovery wallets currently hold about $5.6 million.

Web3Auth, the Consensys-owned service that runs Google and Apple sign-in for crypto wallet Keplr, said it moved funds out of vulnerable Keplr wallets to keep them away from an attacker. The 12 addresses it says hold the recovered funds had about $5.6 million in crypto as of publicatino time on Sunday, according to onchain data reviewed by The Block.

A flaw in Web3Auth's systems exposed the private keys of some Keplr Wallet users who signed up with a Google or Apple account, the firms said. Though it was initially designed for the Cosmos ecosystem, Keplr now supports now supports many non-Cosmos chains, including EVM networks, and said in 2025 that it had more than 2 million users.

"This was not a hack of Keplr's code. The incident originated at Web3Auth, a provider Keplr uses," Keplr co-founder Josh Lee wrote on X, estimating that about 20% of Keplr's Google and Apple login accounts were affected. Users who set up Keplr with a recovery phrase or a hardware wallet are not affected, Keplr said.

Web3Auth said on Saturday it had started emptying vulnerable wallets the attacker hadn't reached yet, using "the same path of the original attacker." "Funds recovered through this process are safe, and we are working with Keplr on a path to recovery for users," Web3Auth said.

Web3Auth's reference to an "original attacker" suggests some wallets were drained before the recovery began. Web3Auth has not said how many wallets the attacker hit or how much was taken, and did not immediately respond to a request for further information from The Block.

"We are aware that some of these transactions have been mistaken for attacker activity," Web3Auth said early Sunday, when it published its recovery addresses. Blockchain analytics firm Bitquery had reported on Saturday that a thief collected about $6 million from more than 44,000 Keplr wallets. All eight addresses Bitquery tied to the thief are on Web3Auth's recovery list.

Most of the recovered funds are on Cosmos Hub, where Web3Auth's recovery address holds about 2.49 million ATOM Cosmos Hub (ATOM), worth roughly $3.3 million. Much of that ATOM had been staked, and the sweeps converted it into tokens that could be moved right away instead of waiting out the network's 21-day withdrawal period.

Lee said on Saturday that many vulnerable accounts may have dodged the attack. "It appears only a fraction of that 20% has actually been exploited, and the remaining accounts may be recoverable," he wrote. Keplr warned that some accounts "may already show a reduced or empty balance as a result of these mitigation efforts."

Keplr told Google and Apple login users not to send any funds to their accounts, advising them to move any remaining funds to a wallet secured by a recovery phrase or a hardware device. Keplr said it is working with Web3Auth on a way for users to check whether their accounts were affected.

Web3Auth first disclosed the issue on Friday afternoon and paused the service that lets apps create wallets through social logins. It said it had found no evidence that users of MetaMask, Consensys' flagship wallet, were affected. Consensys acquired Web3Auth in June 2025 and now markets the service as MetaMask Embedded Wallets, according to its developer documentation.

The Block has reached out to Web3Auth, Consensys and Keplr for comment.

#5108. Strive (ASST) Adds 29 Bitcoin for $2.2 Million as Weekly Buys Slow After Crossing 43,000

Strive bought another 29 bitcoin last week for about $2.2 million at an average of $75,721 per coin between Oct. 31 and Nov. 6, a Monday 8-K filing shows, lifting total holdings to 43,153 BTC from 43,124 as of Oct. 30 and capping a month that had already pushed the Dallas treasury past 40,000 coins.

Chairman and CEO Matthew Cole posted the same figures on X: “Strive acquired 29 $BTC for $2.2M at an average cost of $75,721 per bitcoin, bringing total holdings to ₿43,153.” Strive’s corporate account republished the note minutes later. The average price is inclusive of fees and expenses, the filing says.

The buy is a sharp downshift from the pace Strive set through October. The prior week alone, from Oct. 24 through Oct. 30, Strive added 124 bitcoin at about $80,526 a coin. Between Oct. 1 and Oct. 30 the company acquired 15,124 bitcoin in total, and Friday’s third-quarter results put year-to-date bitcoin yield — Strive’s measure of the percentage change in bitcoin per share — at 63.2% through Sept. 30, with an 18.5% print for the third quarter alone.

Holdings stood at 28,000 bitcoin on Sept. 30 before the October sprint. That climb followed multi-thousand-coin buys earlier this fall, including a 6,182-coin purchase funded with preferred proceeds, and kept Strive among the largest public corporate stacks after Strategy.

Cash and cash equivalents fell to $487.6 million as of Nov. 6 from $490.4 million a week earlier, a $2.8 million drop that roughly covers the roughly $2.2 million bitcoin spend plus fees. Strive did not break out a separate funding line for this week’s coins in the 8-K.

The preferred stack kept moving. Shares of Strive’s 15% Series A perpetual preferred (SATS) rose by 33,141 to 7,036,559, a tally that includes shares sold through 4:00 p.m. Eastern that settle the next business day. SATA preferred stock was unchanged at 14,030,176. Effective common shares outstanding held flat at 127,331,109 after a Class A increase of 15,382 was offset by a matching Class B decline.

Strive still holds 505,000 shares of Strategy’s variable-rate STRC preferred, marked at a fair value of $50.4 million as of Nov. 6, up slightly from $50.1 million a week earlier. The company closed a registered offering of 7 million SATS shares on Oct. 20 for about $609 million in net proceeds and later lined up a $1 billion SATS at-the-market program, tools that have funded the larger buys that built the stack and that still leave room to keep adding if the ATM finds buyers.

At bitcoin near $74,650, the 43,153-coin treasury is worth roughly $3.22 billion on paper. That marks the stack about 1.4% under this week’s $75,721 average purchase price, though Strive has not disclosed a companywide cost basis in Monday’s filing the way Strategy does each week.

The Strive update lands on the same Monday that Strategy disclosed its own weekly purchase. Strategy added 1,036 bitcoin for $81.9 million at an average of $79,012, lifting its holdings to 850,364 BTC bought for $64.16 billion at an average cost of $75,456. The two filings keep the familiar pairing in view: Strategy still dwarfs every other public corporate holder, while Strive has used preferred issuance to climb the treasury rankings with a faster bitcoin-per-share growth claim.

ASST closed Friday at $21.45, up from Thursday’s $20.59 close and still well below its Oct. 21 local high near $30.75. The 52-week range runs from $7.02 to $34.40. At the time of writing, bitcoin is near $74,650, Bitcoin Magazine Pro data shows. The test for Strive from here is whether another week of single-digit coin adds is a pause after October’s sprint or the new run rate once the preferred ATM is the main fuel.

#5106. Onchain data casts doubt on insider-trading theory in NYT’s ‘suspicious’ election betting story

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

A New York Times story raised questions about a Polymarket trader's winning Senate bets, but The Block's analysis reveals a broader trading history that suggests other explanations for the wagers.

The New York Times wrote Friday that Vanderbilt University researchers had flagged large, profitable bets on Polymarket and Kalshi that were placed before certain polls on the Nebraska and Michigan Senate races were released. The polls favored the candidates the trader had backed, raising questions about whether the trader had advance access to the results.

However, The Block's own analysis of onchain records and trading data found that the trader has a long history of both large, risky trades and complex DeFi yield-farming that was unreported by the Times. Matching onchain deposits to Kalshi trades also suggests that the trader may have placed two large bets 10 minutes after one poll in question was published.

The trader held on to the bets all the way to Election Day rather than taking profits from short-term moves in the markets in the wake of the polls, and placed a bet on the race in Michigan while the Times/Siena polling was still underway, facts that the Times acknowledged in its report.

Those facts complicate the theory that the trader's profits resulted from advance access to unpublished Times/Siena polls. Two experts who reviewed The Block's findings said that while foul play can't be definitively ruled out, other explanations, like the trader being a crypto whale with a proclivity for large directional bets, fit the evidence better.

The Times story acknowledged it was unclear whether the trader had inside information. The researchers offered other possible explanations, and Polymarket told the paper it was conducting its own investigation.

Rajiv Sethi, an economics professor at Barnard College who reviewed The Block's findings, said the activity looked more like bets on the eventual election outcomes than an attempt to profit from the publication of the Times/Siena polls.

"I think these were just directional bets. Maybe they were based on non-public information such as privately commissioned surveys, but I don't think the Times/Siena polls were the information source," Sethi said.

The Vanderbilt researchers identified a Polymarket user who bet $191,277 that Sen. Pete Ricketts, a Nebraska Republican, would win re-election. Most of that money was wagered in the early hours of Oct. 31, roughly an hour before a Times/Siena poll published at 5:05 a.m. ET showed Ricketts ahead by six points. The user ultimately made more than $77,000 on the race, according to the Times.

The same user bet $622,608 on a Democratic victory in Michigan's Senate race. At around 7:53 p.m. ET on Nov. 1, the Times published polls of Michigan and six other states showing Michigan Democrat Abdul El-Sayed ahead by 11 points. The user made more than $180,000 on that race when El-Sayed won the seat.

The Block's analysis of Polymarket's public records matched those bets to an account named dubdubdub2. On Oct. 31, it bought $349,135 of "Yes" shares on a Democratic win in Michigan at 3:27 a.m. and $273,473 of "No" shares on a Republican win at 5:20 a.m., which are essentially bets in the same direction. Together, the two bets match the Michigan total reported by the Times.

The bets were unusually large for those markets. The account's Michigan purchases were the largest daily total by one account in any of the 16 Senate races The Block analyzed back to mid-September, and were nearly three times the next largest.

"There's a series of circumstances here that it's hard to write it off as a random occurrence," Brett Goldstein, who co-leads the prediction markets research team at Vanderbilt's Institute of National Security, told the Times. He said the activity "requires further investigation."

But while the Polymarket trades stood out on the platform, The Block's review of the trader's onchain history suggests the size of the bets was not unusual for the trader, and the specific details of some trades cut against the theory that they were exploiting advance knowledge of the polls.

Michigan also wasn't the race the Nov. 1 polls moved most. That release covered seven states, and the biggest market reaction came in Kansas, where Polymarket's odds of a Democratic win jumped from about 52.5% to 60.5% within half an hour. In Michigan, where Democrats were already the strongest favorites at about 74.5%, the odds rose to about 79.5% over two hours.

The trader did not bet on Kansas. Its market was much smaller, however, with about $1.9 million traded on a Democratic win through the election, compared with about $7.9 million in Michigan.

The researchers told the Times that the trader sent large sums to Kalshi and that large bets on similar markets followed, but the report didn't identify those bets.

The Block traced funds from wallets linked to the Polymarket account to a crypto payment provider used by Kalshi, then compared those deposits with Kalshi's public trade records. The records suggest the trader was betting on the same races on both platforms in the same directions, including possible bets placed after the Nebraska poll became public.

The account's Senate bets were funded by a separate wallet that also traded on Hyperliquid. The Block linked that wallet to the one controlling dubdubdub2 through a history of transfers between connected addresses. Its five transfers to dubdubdub2's Polymarket deposit address on Oct. 30 and 31, totaling $850,000, also match the account's deposit records to the dollar.

At 5:29 p.m. ET on Oct. 30, the betting wallet withdrew its remaining $2.2 million from Hyperliquid, where it had been trading over the previous day. About a minute later, it began sending funds to a deposit address that forwards money to a wallet labeled by blockchain analytics firm Arkham and Arbiscan as belonging to Zero Hash.

Kalshi lists Zero Hash among its crypto deposit providers. The betting wallet sent about $1.39 million to the deposit address through Oct. 31. The Block found no earlier transfers to Zero Hash from the trader's known wallets.

Kalshi's data does not link specific trades to specific accounts. However, since the transfers were consistent with Kalshi deposits and six-figure bets were rare in Kalshi's Nebraska and Michigan Senate markets, The Block was able to match certain deposits to trades by timing and size.

Several of the trader's Zero Hash deposits were followed within roughly a minute by large purchases in Kalshi's Nebraska Senate markets. On Oct. 30, a $175,000 deposit at 5:30:08 p.m. was followed 31 seconds later by about $159,000 in purchases backing Ricketts at 66 cents. A further $157,000 deposit at 5:31:58 p.m. was followed at 5:33:29 p.m. by about $169,000 in bets against Osborn at 67 cents. Both positions backed the same election outcome.

At about 5:15 a.m. on Oct. 31, 10 minutes after the Times/Siena poll was published, Kalshi recorded about $153,000 in purchases backing Ricketts to win and independent Dan Osborn to lose. The two groups of trades came eight seconds apart at prices around 75 cents, echoing the paired purchases seen earlier.

These trades are harder to attribute to the trader, however. They came about 50 minutes after a $250,000 deposit, while several earlier purchases followed deposits within roughly a minute. The deposit could also have funded bets in other markets.

If the trader did make those bets, continuing to buy in after the poll was published would suggest it was betting on the election outcome generally rather than trying to profit from the poll's short-term effect on the market, though late buys do not completely rule out the trader having advance knowledge of the poll.

Onchain transfers suggest the trader may have made more from its Kalshi activity than the roughly $258,000 its Polymarket Senate positions earned. On Nov. 4 and 5, its wallet received about $1.98 million from Zero Hash-labeled wallets, roughly $590,000 more than it had sent to Zero Hash in the first place, presumably as Kalshi deposits.

The transfers alone don't show exactly how much the trader made on Kalshi. The difference between the funds deposited and the funds received could reflect winning bets in other markets, trades that are missing from Kalshi's public records or money the trader originally deposited via a bank account.

The Times reported that the user had traded in just three Polymarket markets before October, but its story did not detail what those trades were. The Block's review found those positions were also substantial, and that holding through settlement was a recurring strategy for the trader.

The trader's dubdubdub2 account bet about $1.05 million on Zohran Mamdani winning the 2025 New York City mayoral election, about $102,000 against Andrew Cuomo in the same race and about $131,000 against the San Francisco 49ers winning the 2026 Super Bowl. It held all three positions until the markets resolved. The Block did not identify any bets that were placed shortly before mayoral polls were published.

Vincent Grégoire, a finance professor at HEC Montréal whose research includes insider trading, described the trader's history as "consistent with a wealthy individual (or small trading firm) who likes to take large bets on various platforms."

While the Times story notes that the trades were made early in the morning U.S. time, The Block's review of roughly two years of activity beginning in October 2024 found that 3 a.m. ET was the busiest hour for wallets linked to the trader. The analysis excluded Oct. 29 through Nov. 5, 2026, so the Senate bets themselves did not influence the result. It covered activity on Ethereum, Arbitrum, Base, Polygon, Hyperliquid and Polymarket, plus Solana bridge transfers.

The trader also has extensive experience within decentralized finance outside prediction markets, per The Block's analysis. The linked wallets have repeatedly farmed lending and yield protocols such as Aave and Pendle, adding up to tens of millions of dollars in total transfer volume. They have withdrawn more than $13 million in stablecoins from Kraken since October 2025. A linked Solana wallet also traded memecoins heavily in early 2025, including the official TRUMP token on its launch weekend.

The trader has also seen its share of trades gone wrong. Immediately before the election bets, the betting wallet deposited about $2.99 million in USDC on Hyperliquid and lost about $783,000 over 26 hours, including fees and funding costs, according to Hypurrscan's records for the account. The losses included about $217,000 on HYPE spot trading, $234,000 on a leveraged HYPE long and $311,000 on leveraged Intel perpetual futures positions opened after Intel reported third-quarter results.

The wallet behind dubdubdub2 also bought 60.43 million WLFI tokens for about $906,500 on the first day of the October 2024 public token sale by World Liberty Financial, the crypto venture owned by President Donald Trump's family, a purchase the Times also noted. The purchase made the wallet the largest buyer in the sale that month.

The Times said the purchase accounted for nearly one-third of all tokens sold that October. Onchain records reviewed by The Block instead put its share at about 6% of the roughly 970 million WLFI sold by the project that month, or about 9% of first-day sales. The Block has asked the Times about the discrepancy.

Grégoire said in an interview with The Block that large election bets can reflect a trader's forecasting models, private polls or other sources of information.

"To me, the simplest explanation is that there's no foul play," he said. "It's just someone that has other sources or other models."

Grégoire questioned why a polling employee would place a bet worth hundreds of thousands of dollars ahead of publication and then leave the position open rather than taking the immediate profit. He also noted that some of Polymarket's biggest historical winners made their money through a small number of large presidential election bets.

Sethi also said the use of Kalshi, which verifies traders' identities, suggests "they were not too concerned with concealing their identity."

Though The Block's analysis casts doubt on the idea that the trader had advance access to the Times/Siena polls, the reasons for the trader's bets cannot be established from the data alone.

"It's almost impossible to tell from the data; you have to know who was trading and on what information," Grégoire said, noting that it's reasonable for the institutions involved to investigate whether their processes had been compromised.

A Polymarket spokeswoman told the Times that the company spotted the trades immediately and is investigating. Kalshi declined to comment to the Times.

The Times also reported that spokespeople for the Times and the Siena Research Institute said they took the allegations seriously and planned to review the research. Times spokesperson Charlie Stadtlander said the newspaper prohibits employees from using unpublished coverage to make investments.

Lyle Durbin, CEO of ReconMR, the data collection firm that owns the Siena Research Institute, said employees are bound by confidentiality and nondisclosure agreements prohibiting the use of unpublished data for any purpose, according to the Times.

World Liberty Financial declined to comment through a representative. Kathleen Clark, a spokesperson for Vanderbilt's Institute of National Security, told The Block that Goldstein would not be able to comment. The Block also sought comment from Polymarket, Kalshi, the Times and Siena but has not heard back as of publication time.

The Times' scrutiny comes as prediction markets face broader questions about their level of oversight over their own platforms. Both Kalshi and Polymarket tightened their insider trading rules earlier this year amid scrutiny from the Senate.

In April, the Justice Department charged U.S. Army soldier Gannon Ken Van Dyke with using classified information about the operation to capture former Venezuelan President Nicolás Maduro to make about $409,881 on Polymarket. A former White House teleprompter operator agreed in August to pay $172,000 to settle CFTC charges that he used advance knowledge of Trump's speeches to bet on Kalshi's "mention markets."

#5103. Greece Passes Crypto Tax Law With 10% Gains Levy, 15% on Staking and DeFi

Greece Passes Crypto Tax Law With 10% Gains Levy, 15% on Staking and DeFi

Greece's parliament passed a bill Thursday imposing a 10% capital-gains tax on cryptocurrency profits, marking a shift from a proposal discussed in October to legislation approved by lawmakers.

The measure also puts a 15% tax on income from staking, crypto lending and decentralized finance, or DeFi.

The legislation’s crypto provisions sit inside a wider Finance Ministry package dealing with debt servicing, financial oversight, and government accounting, with the new crypto income-tax rules scheduled for January 1, 2027.

Individual investors with annual net crypto gains of €500 ($557) or less will be exempt. Cross that threshold, though, and the 10% tax applies to the entire gain, rather than just the amount over €500 ($557).

For example, an investor with €600 ($669) in net gains would face a €60 ($67) tax under the new rate, not €10 ($11). The rules generally calculate gains by subtracting the acquisition cost from the value received when the crypto is disposed of.

Selling Bitcoin for euros is one taxable event, but spending crypto on goods or services can also count as a disposal. Investors will need transaction records to establish what they paid for their assets and what those assets were worth when sold or spent.

Yield from staking, lending, and providing liquidity to DeFi protocols is treated differently. Greece will classify those returns as interest income and tax them at 15%, according to the Finance Ministry.

Qualifying crypto losses exceeding €500 ($557) can be carried forward for five years and offset against future crypto gains. The bill also gives investors a 12-month window to voluntarily declare previously unreported crypto income without late-filing penalties or interest, provided they meet its conditions and pay within 60 days of filing.

The passage follows an October public consultation on the proposal.The draft proposed a 10% rate that was already lower than the 15% levy Greek officials had discussed in June.

Athens says exchanging one cryptocurrency for another should not itself trigger a capital-gains tax. But the wording lawmakers approved may not make that exclusion as clear as the government intended.

A review by parliament's Scientific Service flagged the bill's broad language covering disposals made "for consideration." That phrase could include token-to-token trades, the review said, even though the accompanying explanation says such swaps should not create taxable gains.

The reviewers recommended an explicit exemption and suggested clearer rules for exchanges involving stablecoins and electronic-money tokens. Those details matter for traders who rotate between assets without cashing out to fiat currency.

Greece is also preparing the administrative rules needed to put the tax into practice. A senior Finance Ministry official told Reuters that guidance on transaction transparency and tax audits would be issued in the coming weeks.

The government has not estimated how much the tax will raise. Officials say the market is difficult to measure because many Greek crypto investors use trading platforms based overseas.

#5102. US sanctions Canadian money-exchange firms over Iran ties

US also offers as much as $20 million and possible relocation for information about Iran’s nuclear program.

The US Treasury imposed financial sanctions on eight Canadian money-service companies it claims have done business with sanctioned ‌Iranian entities.

These companies facilitated the exchange of Iranian rials and other currencies for digital assets, enabling international transactions, a US Treasury Department statement said on Friday.

The sanctions, which serve to isolate the companies from the dollar-based financial system, are the latest in a series of Treasury moves against firms outside the US.

These businesses have facilitated transactions with sanctioned Iranian digital asset exchanges, as well as with digital asset addresses associated with Iran’s Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC), according to the Treasury.

Related: US plans to seize $1B in crypto linked to Iran this week: Scott Bessent

“Canada cannot be a safe haven for illicit financial facilitators that provide sanctions evasion services to the Iranian regime and its proxies,” said Secretary of the Treasury Scott Bessent. “Treasury will pursue and disrupt the Iranian regime’s financial channels anywhere in the world.”

Cointelegraph reported in August that the Treasury Department’s Office of Foreign Assets Control, or OFAC, was targeting crypto exchanges facilitating the transfer of funds to Iran’s IRGC. Bessent said at the time that the US planned to “increase the economic pressure” on Iran, focusing on financial networks funding the country’s regime “in dollars, rials, or crypto.”

Friday’s designations are a continuation of Treasury’s campaign to “disrupt the regime’s exploitation of digital assets to fund terrorism, human rights abuses, and attacks on US personnel and allies,” the statement said.

It added that the actions were coordinated with FINTRAC, Canada’s financial intelligence unit.

Some of the entities subject to this action have changed their names and addresses to evade detection, the statement noted.

In a separate action on Friday, the US Rewards for Justice program offered as much as $20 million and possible relocation for information about Iran’s nuclear program, including its facilities, equipment and personnel.

Iran’s lack of compliance with the International Atomic Energy Agency on safeguards has led to the agency’s loss of “continuity of knowledge” regarding Iran’s production of enriched uranium and components like centrifuges, the statement said.

#5100. What is Zcash (ZEC)? How shielded transactions work

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Zcash, on the other hand, gives users the option to process their transactions privately. It offers transparent transactions, which function like a normal BTC transaction, as well as shielded transactions, which allow users to keep transaction details private through advanced cryptography.

The network launched in October 2016, and the nonprofit Zcash Foundation was established the following year to support the network and its development. Zcash has since evolved into a decentralized ecosystem spanning multiple independent organizations and contributors. The Zcash Foundation continues to maintain core infrastructure and support protocol development.

Zcash’s shielded transactions make use of cryptographic proofs to verify transactions are legitimate while keeping the underlying information concealed. This means that the transaction amount, sender’s address and recipient’s address are all kept private.

To understand how this works, it helps to look at how Zcash handles private funds. Transactions on the network are processed in two parallel but separate accounting environments: the transparent ledger and shielded pools. The latter is where private transactions take place; shielded pools are parts of the chain where funds can be held and transferred without their ownership, balance and history being publicly visible.

According to figures displayed on the official Zcash network dashboard, approximately 29% of the issued ZEC supply was held in shielded pools as of October 2026. It is also worth noting that transactions moving between transparent and shielded pools can reveal the amounts entering or leaving those pools, which in turn could be used to infer relationships between addresses.

While the regular blockchain uses conventional public addresses, shielded pools instead represent funds as cryptographic records called notes. When a user processes a shielded payment, their wallet uses existing notes to create new ones for the recipient. This transaction is recorded on the Zcash blockchain, but observers cannot see who received the notes, which notes were spent or how much ZEC changed hands.

A zero-knowledge proof (ZKP) is a cryptographic method that allows someone to prove a statement is true without revealing the underlying information used to establish it. Imagine if someone wanted to prove they were over 18 without revealing their exact date of birth — ZKPs are able to achieve a similar effect by using cryptographic mathematics.

Instead of broadcasting all of the information needed to verify a payment, the sender generates a cryptographic proof of its validity. Other users can view and verify that proof — the product of the cryptographic algorithm — while the information used to generate it is kept concealed.

Zcash uses a specific type of proof called a zero-knowledge succinct non-interactive argument of knowledge, or zk-SNARK. It also makes use of cryptographic identifiers called nullifiers attached to notes to prevent double-spending.

Viewing keys introduce another level of control for Zcash users. These allow them to selectively reveal transaction data with third parties while keeping it hidden from the general public. These viewing keys give limited read-only access to the wallet without giving the holder the capability to spend the balance inside.

The network allows for different levels of visibility. For example, an incoming viewing key reveals only received payments, while a full viewing key reveals broader details of an account’s shielded activity.

This is known as selective disclosure, allowing users to choose when and with whom they share financial information. Custom controls like this distinguish Zcash from networks like Monero (XMR), where all activity is anonymous by default.

Zcash supports transparent addresses that operate similarly to Bitcoin addresses and shielded addresses designed to protect transaction details. Unified addresses simplify the process by allowing users to manage both types of transaction from inside one wallet.

Sender, recipient and transaction amounts are publicly visible

zs1 for Sapling; newer shielded receivers are incorporated into unified addresses

Conceals transaction details when used in fully shielded transfers

This convoluted process was streamlined by successive network upgrades:

The upgrade also restricted activity in the original Orchard pool and introduced a mechanism known as a turnstile, allowing existing funds to migrate to Ironwood while enforcing supply-integrity checks. Compatible wallets can handle this migration without requiring users to create a new wallet.

New ZEC coins enter circulation through proof-of-work mining. This is the same system used by Bitcoin, wherein miners dedicate computing power to solving complex mathematical problems, with the successful miners gaining the right to produce new blocks that are in turn validated by other nodes. The specific algorithm used by Zcash is called Equihash.

ZEC also shares a similar emission schedule with BTC, with a fixed supply cap of 21 million coins and a halving in new emissions roughly every four years. The most recent Zcash halving occurred in November 2024, cutting the block subsidy (the network reward paid to miners) to 1.5625 ZEC.

Unlike Bitcoin, however, Zcash also directs a portion of newly minted coins toward network development.  A new funding model was introduced in November 2025 which now sees 80% of block subsidies paid to miners, 8% to community grants and 12% to a development fund controlled by ZEC holders.

Ring signatures, stealth addresses, RingCT

Not supported as a standard transaction mode

It’s important to note that neither ZEC nor XMR guarantee complete anonymity. Zcash balances can be tracked while moving in or out of private pools, while wallets on both networks can potentially be de-anonymized if they interact with a user’s centralized exchange accounts.

No. Shielded transactions can conceal the sender, recipient and amount, but Zcash does not guarantee complete anonymity. Transparent transactions remain publicly visible, while exchange records, network information and user behavior can reveal identifying details.

2. What is the difference between shielded and transparent Zcash transactions?

Transparent transactions publicly reveal wallet addresses and transaction amounts, much like Bitcoin. Shielded transactions use zero-knowledge proofs to conceal this information while allowing the network to verify the payment. Transfers between transparent and shielded pools can still reveal the amounts entering or leaving those pools.

Zcash ownership and use are generally permitted in many jurisdictions, although restrictions vary by country. Some jurisdictions, such as the EU, have introduced legislation restricting cryptocurrency platforms from maintaining anonymous accounts. Although not a blanket ban, this could limit the accessibility of ZEC when it takes effect in July 2027.

Zcash has a maximum supply of 21 million ZEC, matching BTC’s supply cap. New coins are issued through proof-of-work mining, with the block subsidy periodically reduced through halvings. The latest halving occurred in November 2024.

#5097. Treasury Cuts Crypto Exchange MEXC Off From US Financial System

Treasury Cuts Crypto Exchange MEXC Off From US Financial System

The U.S. Treasury Department has moved to sever crypto exchange MEXC Global from the American financial system, accusing it of facilitating at least $16 billion in illicit transactions tied to Iran, North Korea and Russia.

In an order issued Friday through its Financial Crimes Enforcement Network, Treasury said MEXC, one of the world’s 20 largest crypto exchanges, served as a major conduit for sanctions evasion and cybercrime.

The action, part of a broader effort dubbed Operation Economic Outcast, bars U.S. financial institutions from processing any funds transfers to or from the exchange. It takes effect 30 days after publication in the Federal Register.

“Financial institutions cannot have it both ways: Either you support the Iranian regime, or you have access to the U.S. financial system,” Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent said in a statement, vowing to keep severing the regime’s “economic lifelines” and its attempts to use digital assets to dodge sanctions.

Founded in 2018, MEXC processes more than $1 billion in daily transactions, holds around $5 billion in reserves, and offers more than 3,000 tokens, according to FinCEN. Between May 2021 and May 2026, FinCEN said, the platform moved at least $16 billion connected to illicit activity, including nearly $1.5 billion tied to Russian sanctions evasion, with $232 million in direct transactions with sanctioned entities. The agency named MEXC a “primary money laundering concern” in connection with Russian illicit finance.

Despite publicly claiming anti-money-laundering controls and barring users in places like Iran and North Korea, MEXC’s compliance was riddled with “severe deficiencies,” FinCEN said.

The exchange was still transacting with sanctioned Russian exchange Garantex and its successor Grinex as recently as June 2026, more than four years after Garantex was first designated. It also processed roughly $200 million linked to the A7A5 stablecoin and about $22 million benefiting Iran’s central bank and its Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps.

Those connections place MEXC squarely within a sprawling sanctions-evasion ecosystem that Treasury has been dismantling piece by piece.

The department last month designated Russia’s A7 Network a transnational criminal organization and proposed cutting its front companies off from U.S. finance, flagging more than $179 billion in transactions through its ruble-backed A7A5 token, much of it routed through Garantex and Grinex.

The same campaign has reached into Iran’s crypto sector, where Treasury gained sweeping authority to sanction anyone operating in it and blacklisted the Iranian exchange Nobitex over its role in funneling crypto to the IRGC.

The order uses a provision of the Combating Russian Money Laundering Act rather than a standard sanctions designation, a tool FinCEN has increasingly reached for to cut off crypto infrastructure that operates outside the correspondent banking system.

#5094. Bitcoin holders have received eCash — What can you do with it?

Bitcoin’s eCash fork gives eligible holders an equivalent balance of ECX, but questions remain over where they can trade the tokens and what they can do with them.

Somewhere between the Binance hack and the US midterms, Paul Sztorc’s long-awaited Bitcoin fork, known as the eCash network, went live.

“We need to actively sabotage the ‘degrowth’ policies killing Bitcoin,” Sztorc tells Magazine.

The hard fork gives eligible Bitcoin holders an interesting windfall: their equivalent BTC balance in the new chain’s native token, ECX.

So what benefits does eCash bring to developers, and what can Bitcoin HODLers actually do with their new coins?

Developed by Sztorc’s LayerTwo Labs, eCash is a separate hard fork of Bitcoin born out of Sztorc’s frustration with some of the “complacency narratives” and “cult-like toxicity” he believes has been holding Bitcoin back.

Sztorc dismisses criticism of Drivechains’ security. Source: archive.ecash

Its main innovation is Drivechains, a system that gives developers freedom to build independent sidechains with different features without having to make changes to Bitcoin’s base-layer protocol (or in this case, eCash’s base layer).

Five sidechains launched alongside eCash’s mainnet: BitNames, BitAssets, Thunder, Truthcoin and zSide, with two more, the quantum-resistant Photon and decentralized exchange CoinShift, still to come.

Related: Blockchain developer Paul Sztorc announces BTC hard fork eCash

Sztorc has spent over a decade advocating for Drivechains and believes that Bitcoin’s notoriously conservative development approach has held back its potential.

“Anything that isn’t maximizing network usage is simply a distraction,” he says. “Users, the ones who actually pay transaction fees to miners, will win in the long run.”

The eCash fork took place at Bitcoin block 973,728, and anyone who controlled their private keys at the snapshot received an equivalent ECX balance.

“If you held the keys to your BTC at that exact block, you now own the exact same amount of ECX,” LayerTwo Labs tells Magazine. “It is that simple, and you can access them right now.”

Like beauty, simplicity is in the eye of the beholder, and Bitcoiners who self-custody their coins will need to use a wallet that supports eCash and carefully follow its process to separate the ECX from their BTC.

Because the two networks share transaction history, an unprotected transaction on one chain could be replayed on the other.

“Never move your BTC first, as an ordinary, unprotected BTC spend can easily be replayed on the ECX network,” LayerTwo Labs warns. Replay controls are built into the protocol, it says, but the company advises holders to move ECX first, before spending BTC.

Anyone keeping BTC on a centralized exchange is “at their mercy regarding distribution,” and should check whether their exchange supports the fork or has announced an ECX distribution.

Accessing ECX isn’t the same thing as being able to sell it and the tokens are not yet widely available on larger crypto exchanges. Depending on which country you are in, there can be tax implications from Bitcoin forks, which are worth looking up before filing your next tax return.

NeoxEX said it opened native ECX deposits, withdrawals and trading on Oct. 29, with ECX/USDT, ECX/USDC and ECX/BTC pairs, while CoinW announced plans to distribute ECX to eligible Bitcoin holders, with deposits, withdrawals and trading to be announced at a later date.

Orca, a decentralized exchange on Solana, lets users swap USDC for wrapped ECX (wECX) through a liquidity pool. That gives holders another way to trade exposure to ECX, but it involves a wrapped token on a different blockchain rather than native ECX trading on the eCash network.

Related: Binance, Polymarket seek Polygon hard fork to recover $6.8M in stolen crypto

How much are the tokens worth? CoinMarketCap placed ECX at $32.63, with around $355,000 in 24-hour trading volume as of Nov. 5, while CoinGecko shows the token as a preview listing, with no price yet available and no tracked exchange markets. Crypto market data provider Kaiko says it does not track ECX, which limits the availability of independent market data.

LayerTwo Labs says early trading saw ECX fluctuate between $40 and $50, but it did not provide supporting order book depth or trading volume figures, pointing instead to the Orca pool’s total value locked as a way for users to assess available liquidity.

eCash is $32.63 on CoinMarketCap as of Nov. 5

“If our fork replicates even a fraction of [Bitcoin Cash] BCH’s initial performance,” Sztorc says, “the liquidity will be staggering,” adding that anyone concerned about low liquidity can hold their coins and decide later.

“We are actively adding new exchanges and trading pairs, so the user experience will only improve,” he says. “Otherwise, you’re welcome to take your free money and leave.”

Sztorc says the value of eCash will ultimately depend on whether developers build useful applications and users are willing to pay for them. Paying customers and transaction fee revenue are the project’s main measures of success during its first year, he says.

He’s not concerned about criticism of Drivechains’ security model, especially the “superstition” that miners could coordinate to steal funds held on sidechains.

“If you genuinely believe miners will torch their own billion-dollar operations for a short-term theft on an L2, then you shouldn’t trust Bitcoin’s L1 either,” he says.

So for now, eCash gives Bitcoiners a new asset and developers a new playground — but whether ECX becomes much more than that will depend on whether anyone actually wants to use it.
