#5018. Binance, Tether and Trump crypto firms face Senate records deadline

Sen. Richard Blumenthal asked four crypto firms to start sending records by Nov. 30 and warned of further action if they miss the Jan. 4 deadline.

US Senator Richard Blumenthal gave Binance, Tether and two Trump-linked crypto firms until Jan. 4, 2027, to voluntarily turn over records sought in Senate inquiries.

Failure to meet the January deadline could prompt further action, he warned.

Blumenthal, the ranking Democrat and incoming chair of the Senate Permanent Subcommittee on Investigations, sent follow-up letters Thursday to Binance, Tether, World Liberty Financial and Fight Fight Fight LLC, the company that launched President Donald Trump’s TRUMP memecoin.

The letters asked the companies to begin turning over records by Nov. 30. The Jan. 4 deadline falls a day after Democrats are due to take control of the Senate.

Blumenthal wrote that the subcommittee would consider “alternative measures to obtain compliance” if the companies miss the January deadline. The panel’s current rules allow its chair to issue subpoenas for documents and records.

Cointelegraph sought comment from Binance, Tether, World Liberty Financial and Fight Fight Fight LLC but did not receive responses before publication.

Related: US senator probes Tether after reported deal with ex-Trump crypto adviser

In May 2025, Blumenthal opened an inquiry into potential conflicts of interest involving Trump’s crypto ventures. He sought records from both firms on their ownership, foreign investments and safeguards against conflicts of interest. World Liberty Financial’s lawyers disputed his claims and said the company had no affiliation with Fight Fight Fight LLC.

In June 2026, Blumenthal and four other Senate Democrats called for hearings into a reported $500 million deal for an Abu Dhabi investment firm to buy a 49% stake in World Liberty Financial.

In February, Blumenthal asked Binance for records on transactions linked to Iranian entities and Russia’s shadow fleet. Binance called the underlying reports false in its March reply and said that, to its knowledge, no Binance account had transacted directly with an Iran-based entity. In Thursday’s letter, Blumenthal said Binance still hadn’t turned over any of the requested records.

Blumenthal’s letter to Tether renewed requests for records on the use of USDT by sanctioned entities and on Tether’s dealings with Nxum, a consulting firm co-founded by former White House crypto adviser Bo Hines.

In September, Blumenthal released a report by the panel’s Democratic staff alleging that USDT has become a key part of Iran’s shadow banking network. Tether disputed claims that USDT provides a haven for sanctioned actors, saying it helped authorities freeze nearly $550 million in Iran-linked tokens in 2026.

#5017. The 2027 Kia EV6 is over $8,000 more than the new EV3

Kia announced that 2027 EV6 prices start at about $38,000, or $8,000 more than the new EV3.

The 2027 Kia EV6 arrives as a carryover model from last year. After reducing prices on the 2026 model year by $5,000 just a few months ago, the 2027 EV6 remains the same price across all trims.

The base EV6 Light standard-range RWD model starts at $37,900, not including the $1,545 destination fee, which is the same as last year.

Powered by a 63 kWh battery, the standard-range variant offers up to 237 miles of range. Upgrading to the EV6 Light long range RWD model costs an extra $3,300, starting at $41,200, but delivers up to 319 miles of range using a larger 84 kWh battery.

The 84 kWh battery offers up to 319 miles of range in RWD variants, while AWD EV6 models get up to 295 miles. The top-spec EV6 GT-Line AWD has up to 270 miles of range.

Kia said that with the EV6 MSRP unchanged and the launch of the new EV3, it’s “making EVs more attainable for those who embrace the fully electric lifestyle.”

The 2027 Kia EV3 starts at just $29,890, or about $8,000 less than the EV6. Kia’s entry-level electric SUV is available in five trims: Light, Wind, Land, GT-Line, and GT.

The lowest-priced EV3 Light FWD model uses a 58.3 kWh battery and delivers an EPA-estimated range of up to 221 miles.

Higher-priced trims, including the Wind, Land, GT-Line, and GT, use a larger 81.4 kWh battery that delivers up to 321 miles of range on a single charge. Like the EV6, the EV3 is also available with front-wheel-drive and all-wheel-drive powertrain options.

Inside, both the EV6 and EV3 feature Kia’s ccNC infotainment system. The setup includes nearly 30″ of screen space in a curved panoramic display, including dual 12.3″ instrument cluster and central navigation screens, plus a 5″ climate control touchscreen.

Both also include a native NACS port so that you can access Tesla Superchargers without a separate adapter.

However, the EV6 uses an 800V architecture, enabling faster charging than the EV3, which uses a 400V system.

I've had four EVs and the 800v charging system on my 2025 Ioniq 5 is the biggest improvement I have experienced in any EV. My road trip stops are now about 20 minutes to go from 15% to 85% at fast chargers. To me, this is a game changer. When people talk about range anxiety, they are really talking about long recharging times and with these speeds, you'll be back on the road in the time it takes to hit the restroom and grab a bit to eat.

The EV6 can charge from 10% to 80% in about 18 minutes, whereas the EV3 takes about 30 minutes.

The EV6 is also larger and less boxy than the EV3, measuring 184.8″ long, 74″ wide, and 60.8″ tall, with a 114.2″ wheelbase. In comparison, the EV3 is 169.3″ long, 72.8″ wide, and 62″ tall, with a 105.5″ wheelbase.

Which one would you drive? The new EV3, the EV6, or would you go with Kia’s three-row EV9? Let us know in the comments.

With the new 2027 models arriving, Kia is offering a $10,000 customer cash discount on the 2026 EV9, $5,000 on the 2026 EV6, and $1,500 on the 2027 EV3 to clear inventory. You can use the links below to see what’s available near you.

#5013. Lectric launches XP Lite3 e-bike, the new best low-cost model in the US

Lectric eBikes, the largest electric bike company in the US by just about any metric you can measure, has just unveiled its latest model: the Lectric XP Lite3. And with a starting price of just $799, the new lightweight folding electric bike is continuing the company’s tradition of packing surprisingly impressive features into remarkably affordable e-bikes.

The XP Lite2 had been widely considered the best bang-for-your-buck option in the ultra-budget category, offering an affordable yet punchy Class 2 e-bike for a song, despite still coming from a reputable US-based company with strong reputation for warranty and support.

Now, the third-generation model builds on what has become one of the most successful budget e-bike platforms in the country, bringing a long list of upgrades designed to make the bike easier to ride, fold, transport, and live with.

And perhaps most importantly, Lectric has managed to keep that familiar $799 starting price that debuted on the original XP Lite1 all the way back in 2022.

The new Lectric XP Lite3 weighs just 49 lb (22.2 kg), making it one of the lighter options in Lectric’s extensive lineup of folding electric bikes. That’s still no featherweight, partly due to Lectric’s insistence on not neutering the bike’s performance, but it’s still a solid 10-20 lb lighter than the average direct-to-consumer e-bike these days.

The bike retains the compact folding design that made its predecessors popular with everyone from college students to RV owners, but introduces a redesigned frame with improved ergonomics and a reinforced folding latch borrowed from the company’s flagship XP4 model.

New quick-release pedals further reduce the bike’s storage footprint, making it easier to squeeze into a car trunk, apartment closet, or RV storage compartment.

Despite its compact dimensions, the XP Lite3 can support a maximum payload of 275 lb (125 kg) and accommodates riders ranging from 4’8” to 6’2”.

The bike reaches speeds of up to 20 mph (32 km/h) and offers up to 40 miles (64 km) or 80 miles (128 km) of range in Eco mode, though expect significantly less range when using higher-power pedal assist or throttle-only riding. The range discrepancy comes from two different battery offerings. The base model features a 374 Wh battery, while the long-range version has nearly twice the capacity at 672 Wh.

Power comes from Lectric’s Stealth M24 motor, which is rated at 300W continuous but over 800 peak watts. The motor combines with Lectric’s pedal assist selector to offer five levels of pedal assist that are personalizable, letting riders dial in exactly how much boost they’d like on their ride. Riders also get a twist throttle, a more comfortable style of throttle enabled by the lack of shifters crowding the bars on this single-speed e-bike.

Several other upgrades help the XP Lite3 punch above its weight class, including integrated rear turn signals, a bright 3.5-inch full-color display brought over from other recently launched models in the Lectric lineup, and a redesigned LevelUp Lite rear rack with new mounting possibilities.

Stopping power comes from Star Union 602 hydraulic disc brakes with thicker 2.3 mm rotors, another welcome feature on a bike at this price point.

Lectric is also offering a Gates Carbon Belt Drive version of the XP Lite3, giving riders a cleaner, quieter, and virtually maintenance-free alternative to a traditional chain drivetrain. That Gates belt drive model fetches a $100 premium, but is likely worth it for those who value the cleaner, maintenance-free alternative that is likely to outlast the bike itself.

The chain-drive version comes in Arctic White, Blue Mist, Matte Grey, and Matte Lavender, while the belt-drive model is offered in JW Black and Ballerina Pink.

It’s difficult to overstate just how much Lectric has changed the American electric bicycle industry.

The Phoenix, Arizona-based company has grown into the country’s largest e-bike brand by both sales volume and the total number of e-bikes on the road. In doing so, it has become one of the most recognizable names in affordable electric transportation.

But Lectric didn’t get there by simply building cheap electric bikes.

Instead, the company helped revolutionize what consumers should expect from a budget-friendly e-bike, combining capable hardware, practical designs, and aggressive pricing with something that has historically been missing from much of the low-cost e-bike market: a real American company standing behind the product.

While countless inexpensive e-bikes sold online come from little-known overseas brands with questionable long-term support, Lectric has built its reputation around US-based customer service, accessible replacement parts, and a company that riders can actually reach when something goes wrong.

That’s a big part of what has made the XP Lite series so important.

Since its introduction, the XP Lite series has arguably set the standard for what an entry-level electric bike should be. It isn’t necessarily the most powerful, fastest, or fanciest e-bike on the road, but it delivers the essentials at a price that opens electric cycling to an entirely new audience.

And importantly, it does so without requiring riders to gamble on an unknown brand.

The XP Lite3 continues that formula, adding features like hydraulic disc brakes, integrated turn signals, and a color display that would have been difficult to imagine on a $799 e-bike just a few years ago.

I’ve been following Lectric since the company’s earliest days, and it’s been fascinating to watch how the brand has consistently raised the bar for affordable electric bikes. I rode one of Lectric’s first 10 bikes off their nascent production line all the way back in 2019, and I think they’d agree that few people outside the company know the brand better than I do. So I can say that, without a doubt, this is one of the company’s most important launches to date because of how it revolutionizes how much bike someone can get without stretching their budget farther than they have to. And at a time when inflation is still rising unchecked and fuel prices are through the roof, this is what the industry needs more than ever.

Keep in mind that the $799 price tag for the XP Lite3 announced today is the same price that the original XP Lite carried back in 2022, despite that value ballooning to $939 in 2026 dollars due to rising inflation.

What impresses me most isn’t necessarily any single feature on the new XP Lite3. It’s the combination of all those features at a price that remains accessible to so many riders.

Hydraulic brakes, a color display, integrated turn signals, a refined folding mechanism, and even the option for a Gates belt drive? That’s quite a lot of bike for the money. And remember, the performance is still there. This is still a 48V e-bike with a legit 20 mph (32 km/h) top speed on throttle and pedal assist.

Sure, there are lighter e-bikes out there, and there are certainly more premium models with fancier components. But that’s not really the point of the XP Lite.

The point is to make electric biking accessible without making it feel like a compromise at every turn.

And when you consider that you’re getting all of this from the biggest e-bike company in the country, complete with US-based support and a proven track record, it’s hard to argue with the value proposition.

The original XP Lite helped establish what a good affordable electric bike could be. The XP Lite3 looks ready to move that benchmark forward once again.

And on a personal note, not only do I want my own Lectric XP Lite3 now, but I think my wife’s new e-bike will soon be an XP Lite3 in Ballerina Pink with a Gates belt drive. Remember, “Happy wife, happy life!”

#5012. The Man Behind Fashion’s Most Dazzling Spectacles Turns His Attention to the Art of Light

For as long as he can remember, Alexandre de Betak has been entranced by light. As a 10-year-old, he rewired his Paris bedroom so that visitors had to be buzzed in, greeted by shifting colored lights. He went on to found Bureau Betak, a set design agency that transformed the runway with its megawatt spectacles for clients like Dior, Gucci, Jacquemus, and Fendi. De Betak estimates that he produced “thousands” of fashion shows—including Saint Laurent’s spring-summer 2020 ready-to-wear extravaganza, for which he bathed the Eiffel Tower in golden light, and installations for Rodarte featuring fluorescent and neon tubes.

A new truss light sculpture by Alexandre de Betak, seen here in a 17th-century Swedish castle

Now, de Betak, who sold his company in 2021, is turning the spotlight on his own creative practice. On November 5, he opened his first solo exhibition of artworks at Salon 94 in New York. The electronically programmed Truss Light Sculptures are inspired by the scaffolding and fixtures of his early runway shows and in some cases literally made from them. “That equipment is old-fashioned now, but I wanted to honor it,” de Betak says. “And I liked the irony that I spent so much energy over the years trying to hide it.”

On view at Salon 94’s Upper East Side town house through December 19, the exhibit presents two series. In the gallery’s contemporary space, the first is composed of freestanding and wall-mounted aluminum structures fitted with LED bulbs. The upstairs Stone Room, a historic space with Caen stone plaster walls and arched windows, is the backdrop for a second series of versions in bronze. To make them, de Betak had a French foundry recast each part down to the smallest screw by hand, a process that sometimes required more than 20 molds.

A pair of wall-mounted sculptures in aluminum

The light sculptures, which vary from sconce-like creations to hulking constructions out of a sci-fi movie, have a sly sense of humor and a human quality. This is especially true when they come to life, thanks to a remote-control-operated system that activates light sequences that glow, sparkle, thrum, or react to ambient noise, “whether a whisper,” de Betak says, “or extremely loud music.” For these effects, he drew on his stage work, where he was renowned for cueing, or choreographing, light and sound. “I’m interested in the ephemeral nature of what this equipment was made to show—whether a fashion show or a ballet,” he says. “At a moment when everything is virtual, I think it’s more important than ever to create things that produce a live emotion.”

Salon 94’s founder Jeanne Greenberg Rohatyn observes that de Betak’s sculptures are equally powerful switched on or off. Unlit they remind her of David Smith artworks or Russian constructivism. Lit, and with synced sound, they evoke the theatricality of stage performers. “Alex has spent his career building structures that disappear into someone else’s spectacle,” she says. “Here, the structure is the whole point.”

#5009. Ford’s new sub-$30K electric truck can power your home for a lot less

Ford wants to make powering your home with an EV a lot cheaper. Its upcoming sub-$30,000 Fathom electric truck will feature a new bidirectional charging system that ditches the need for an expensive external inverter.

Arriving in 2027, the Fathom will be the first EV from a US automaker priced below $30,000 that can deliver more than 10 kW of AC power to a home without a separate wall-mounted inverter.

And Ford says it’s cutting the cost of the other equipment needed for home backup power, too.

EV batteries store electricity as direct current (DC), but our homes use alternating current (AC). To send power from an EV battery into a house, traditionally, that meant installing a pricey external inverter.

Ford has come up with a different approach for its new Universal EV Platform, which will underpin the Fathom.

After acquiring startup Auto Motive Power in 2023, Ford redesigned its bidirectional charging system from the ground up. Instead of relying on a separate wall-mounted inverter, it built one directly into the truck’s E-box, one of the vehicle’s five main modules.

Fewer parts mean lower costs, and Ford says the savings will extend to the home charging equipment, too.

Fathom owners will have two ways to use the truck’s battery to power their homes.

Home Power Management will let owners charge their truck when electricity rates are low, then use that stored power at home when rates are higher. That could mean real savings for households on time-of-use electricity plans. They’ll need Ford’s new Home Power Station, which the company expects to cost about half as much as its first-generation bidirectional charger.

Home Backup Power will let the truck keep the lights on during an outage. For that, owners will need both the Home Power Station and Ford’s Home Backup Panel. Ford says the backup hardware should cost at least 60% less than its first-generation equipment.

Ford hasn’t released prices for either piece of hardware, so we don’t yet know what a complete home installation will cost.

But pairing a sub-$30,000 electric truck with substantially cheaper home power equipment could make bidirectional charging a lot more appealing.

Making the hardware cheaper is one thing. Making sure utilities can handle thousands of EVs that send electricity back into homes and potentially the grid is another.

Ford formed a working group in July with American Electric Power, BC Hydro, Consumers Energy, Exelon, and Pacific Gas and Electric. They’re working on making it easier for customers to connect bidirectional EVs before the Fathom arrives next year.

For example, someone using their truck solely for home backup power during an outage shouldn’t have to go through the same approval process as someone sending electricity to the grid.

The group is also looking at how to update utility procedures to accommodate newer bidirectional charging standards and make it easier for EV owners to participate in virtual power plants (VPPs). In other words, the batteries in parked EVs could become a useful source of electricity when the grid needs it most.

Ford says the Fathom’s system is designed to meet applicable UL 1741 requirements and IEEE 1547 interconnection standards. It also uses SAE J3068 communications technology, which Ford says helps reduce charger costs.

The idea is to get the technical requirements and utility processes sorted out before affordable bidirectional EVs start arriving in much larger numbers.

Affordable EVs already offer some form of bidirectional power. Two of the cheapest electric cars in the US, the Chevy Bolt and Nissan Leaf, are both available for under $30,000.

The Bolt supports vehicle-to-home (V2H) power through GM Energy equipment, while the Leaf offers vehicle-to-load (V2L) capability through an external device plugged into its charging port.

Ford’s system takes things much further. It can deliver more than 10 kW of AC power to a home without an expensive external inverter, and Ford says the rest of the equipment will cost considerably less, too.

That’s a pretty compelling proposition, especially for people who live in areas prone to power outages or have electricity plans with higher peak-hour rates.

When power outages last a while the area still affected shrinks. While a whole county may be out day one, by day 3 it may just be your neighborhood left. At that point you could drive across town and recharge the car and be good to power your house for another few days.

There are still some unknowns. Ford hasn’t announced the final hardware prices, and installation costs will vary. Plus, utility rules need to catch up if EV owners are going to take full advantage of bidirectional charging.

But this seems to be going in the right direction. If automakers can bring costs down and the industry can agree on consistent standards, having an EV that doubles as a home battery could eventually become a normal part of EV ownership rather than an expensive bonus feature.

#5008. BYD names its ultra-luxury EV, and it could be a game changer next week

BYD’s new flagship EV will be its largest and most luxurious electric sedan yet, potentially debuting two firsts for the company next week: Rolls-Royce-style coach doors and solid-state batteries.

BYD is better known for its low-cost electric cars, like the Seagull, a little hatchback that sells for under $10,000 in China.

The Chinese auto giant has greatly expanded its portfolio over the past few months and years. BYD now offers everything from entry-level EVs to three-row flagship SUVs, pickup trucks, electric supercars, and just about everything in between.

BYD has three sub-brands: Denza, FangChengBao, and Yangwang. It also has two main families under the BYD brand: the Dynasty and Ocean series.

Yangwang sits at the top of BYD’s brand hierarchy and showcases its latest breakthrough technologies.

Previously, Yangwang vehicles debuted on BYD’s quad-motor e4 platform, delivering a whopping 1,300 horsepower and featuring its DiSus-X intelligent body control system, which enables the vehicle to “dance,” jump, and drive on three wheels.

For its next model, BYD is pushing further into the elite segment to take on ultra-luxury brands like Rolls-Royce, Bentley, and Mercedes-Maybach.

BYD revealed the name of its flagship ultra-luxury EV for the first time on Tuesday: the Yangwang Altior, along with a new teaser of the partially uncovered executive sedan.

Coming soon#YANGWANG #YANGWANGALTIOR pic.twitter.com/kFnu6X6csq

Yangwang general manager Hu Xiaoqing said the name was chosen as an independent Chinese-English naming scheme. In China, it goes by DingYao. The “Ding” symbolizes stability and endurance, while “Yao” signifies radiant brilliance, or shining brightly like a star in the sky.

The English name Altior comes from Latin for “higher,” representing Yangwang’s relentless pursuit of ever-greater heights.

The new teaser comes after Hu revealed several images of the new Yangwang executive sedan testing for the first time last month.

Although it was disguised, it appeared to be about the same shape and size as the Mercedes-Benz Maybach S-Class or Rolls-Royce Phantom. Local reports indicate it will be around 5,500 mm (216″) long.

HU confirmed for the first time last week that the global launch of the new flagship sedan will take place in the UAE, not in BYD’s home market of China.

BYD has not yet revealed a specific launch date, but the Yangwang sedan could make its first appearance as early as next week. According to CarNewsChina, citing “sources familiar with the project,” BYD is aiming to reveal the new flagship model in Dubai, UAE, around November 5.

Zheng Yu, Yangwang’s executive general manager, said the new model will feature “unprecedented disruptive technology,” but didn’t reveal specifics.

Although details are still limited, the ultra-luxury sedan was shown at BYD’s Flash Charging station, suggesting it will support megawatt flash charging.

It also features rear-hinged coach doors, a first for BYD. The flagship EV will reportedly be the first to feature BYD’s solid-state batteries.

BYD executive vice president Stella Li said during an interview with Carwow.es last month that the company’s first vehicle to use solid-state batteries will debut in 2027.

Li said BYD is “in the leading position” in solid-state battery technology and its commercialization, and the company plans to prove it next year.

BYD’s battery unit, FinDreams, plans to begin production of sulfide-based all-solid-state batteries in 2027. It will initially be produced in limited batches, then scale to mass production closer to the end of the decade.

The part about solid state batteries got my attention. I'm looking forward to learning more about how they perform!

While BYD has yet to confirm it, introducing the new battery tech in a flagship model would make sense.

The executive sedan is expected to debut around November 5. Prices are rumored to start in the 2 million yuan range, or about $300,000.

Yangwang’s current electric sedan, the U7, starts at 658,000 yuan ($98,000). The brand also sells the U8 (and U8L), a plug-in hybrid luxury off-road SUV, and the U9, a fully electric super-coupe.

The extended U8L is Yangwang’s highest-priced mass-produced vehicle, starting at 1.45 million yuan ($217,000).

Bitcoin price action fell to its lowest levels since mid-August as Nvidia reports added to sell-side pressure across stock-market futures.

#5005. How to Organize a Kitchen Pantry in 6 Easy Steps, According to Experts

A pantry is among the most hardworking areas of the home, but without proper skills, it can also turn into the most cluttered. Learning how to organize a kitchen pantry like a pro requires a pro, so we tapped expert organizers to share their best pantry organization tips. Ahead, you’ll find a step-by-step guide to organizing your pantry—and keeping it that way. The process includes decluttering expired items, grouping pantry staples by category, deciding what to place on upper and lower shelves, and utilizing food storage containers and labeled bins. Whether you have a spacious walk-in pantry or you’re working with limited shelf space, use these pantry organization ideas to maximize vertical space, function, and style.

Start by removing everything from your pantry. “As you are taking the items out, take note of expiration dates and toss anything that is past its best by date, that is stale, or is no longer edible,” says Megan Barnhart, a professional organizer and founder of Barnhart Home. Since the pantry is rarely empty, take the opportunity to deep-clean it—wipe pull-out shelves, wash pantry storage vessels, and sweep the floor. You can also use this time to place liners or contact paper on the shelves. “Liners prevent damage to the wood and paint and make future clean-ups so much easier,” says Catherine Benson, a cleaning and organization aficionado based in Lexington, Kentucky.

Removing everything from the pantry will help you survey what you have in order to organize it better.

Before you return everything into the pantry, group items by category. There are several ways to do this, and experts recommend choosing the method that best suits you. One idea is to group like items together. “Think of your pantry as your own personal grocery store—canned goods together, pastas together, baking ingredients together,” Barnhart shares.

Another option is to categorize items by how you shop and plan meals. “If you shop with specific meals in mind and like to meal plan, group those ingredients together; for example, create a dinner zone that includes individual categories or bins such as pasta, grains, and soup,” says Lisa Eckerle, a professional organizer and owner of The Designer Organizer. If you shop by stocking up on pantry goods, categories like canned goods, baking supplies, and condiments may make more sense. “We normally use a hybrid approach for most of our clients, creating zones based on activity and categories within the zones based on food type,” Eckerle shares.

Once you’ve organized your categories, you might want containers to hold them. But before purchasing any storage vessels, measure pantry shelves, noting their width, depth, and height. Barnhart and Eckerle recommend incorporating the following storage solutions to maximize space and add style.

Risers are one of Barnhart’s favorite ways to store canned goods. “It makes it much easier to see the canned items in the back as well as what is in the front, and I feel it also helps avoid over-buying or repurchasing because you can see what you actually have,” she says.

Turntables are a must-have for pro organizers. “With all L-shaped pantries, we always start by placing turntables in most of the corners; these help maximize that space, avoid food getting lost or spoiled in the back, and create a cohesive visual,” Eckerle says. She notes they also work well on pantry shelves above eye level.

Consider containers and other tools to help store items in your pantry more effectively.

Turntables are great for oils and vinegars, sauces, condiments, broths, and spices. “If you have items you use daily, like nut butters, honey, and spreads that do not require refrigeration, consider putting those items on their own lazy Susan as well; it makes it easy to grab what you need and also makes it easy to clean up small spills and drips because they are contained to the lazy Susan,” Barnhart says.

Barnhart suggests using beautiful baskets or clear containers to group together things like snacks and dry goods. “This contains the items so they are not spilling out onto the shelves or floor and it is also much more aesthetically appealing as well,” Barnhart says.

One of Eckerle’s favorite recent hacks are wire cooling racks on the interior bottom of wooden storage bins. “By adding a plastic shelf liner under the cooling rack for easy cleaning, we created a unique fruit bin that was able to match the rest of the pantry’s decor and still store the fruit correctly with proper airflow,” she shares.

Now it’s time to add everything back into the pantry. “The most-reached-for categories should be near the door or center, with back stock and lesser-used category items placed in the back or the sides,” Eckerle says. She suggests putting meal-related items in the most accessible location, especially breakfast items, so they’re easy to grab when you’re in a rush.

Avoid storing seldom-used or heavy, small appliances on the top shelf or floor. “To prevent injury, damage, and frustration, those items should be stored between knee and shoulder height,” Eckerle says. Additionally, consider at what height you want each category. For example, “whether you want items out of reach of young children or dogs, or accessible and at eye level for children,” Eckerle says.

Keep things you grab often towards the front, while less-used items can go in the back.

“I am a big fan of labeling in the pantry—it helps systems stay in place when everyone knows what goes where and where to put it back,” Barnhart says. A label maker works great for this, but even stickers and sharpies will do.

Once you know how to organize a kitchen pantry, it’s important to maintain it. Eckerle recommends lightly resetting the pantry after every grocery haul. Additionally, she advises scheduling a day each quarter for a thorough pantry reset. “Re-check expiration dates, dispose of open food, and throw away or donate the items/categories that are no longer on your menu,” she says. You can also use this time to consider any seasonal menu changes that may impact how you organize your pantry. “Depending on the season, you may purchase different ingredients or cook different types of meals,” says Shweta Thota, a lifestyle content creator and Intel hardware engineer based in Sacramento. “This may require rearranging your pantry to accommodate seasonal items.” For example, in the winter, you may want to have a setup that works for more canned goods.

#5002. Robinhood opens access to US IPOs for UK retail investors

Robinhood’s UK customers can request shares in eligible US initial public offerings at the offer price before the stocks begin trading.

Robinhood has launched a service that lets UK retail investors request shares in eligible US initial public offerings (IPOs) before trading begins.

Customers can use their existing investment accounts to review offering documents in the app and request shares at the IPO price without paying trading commissions, Robinhood said Monday.

Robinhood said it has participated in more than 50 IPOs since launching the service in the US in 2021. The company did not identify which upcoming US IPOs would be available to UK investors.

Cointelegraph asked Robinhood for clarification but did not receive a response by publication.

The expansion follows Robinhood’s third-quarter earnings report in October, when the company reported record revenue of $1.42 billion and net income of $476 million.

Robinhood shares (HOOD) closed at $80.91 on Friday, gaining 3.2%, according to Yahoo Finance. The stock traded at $79.50 in premarket trading on Monday.

#5000. 18 Best Leather Sofas of 2026: Couches That Are Editor-Approved

The best leather sofas adhere to the ABCs of great seating: agnostic design, beautiful texture, and classic silhouettes. Generally, genuine leather is lauded for its durability and longevity—it’s only going to look better with age and some patina. The fabric also makes a wildly comfy, easy-to-clean surface where dirt and dust can be wiped away with a cloth.

If you’re on the search for a new sofa that radiates sophistication (and not just single energy), there are a bounty of leather options at every price point, whether you prefer the softness of suede or the supple, lived-in feel of top-grain leather. Ahead, we gathered our favorite leather couches available online, from small-space designs to larger sectionals that will become the anchor for your future hangouts.

We also included a few wish-list-worthy collectible pieces from midcentury-modern makers like Tobia Scarpa and de Sede, in case contemporary silhouettes don’t quite measure up to the look you’re going for. Below, some of the most handsome leather styles on our wishlist.

Castlery Jonathan top-grain leather sofa is overstuffed, inviting, and supportive—the holy trinity of comfort. We love the inviting modular design that’s perfect for kicking your feet up on the nearby coffee table or simply lounging around in your modern living space. Rachel Fletcher, AD commerce director, has had the Castlery’s Jonathan sectional since 2022 and still gives it an A. The sofa is streamlined—it’s legless look resembles a seat you might find at one of those dine-in theaters. It’s overstuffed and inviting, but still supportive. Contributor Sarah Madaus said it was so comfortable, she asked her partner to cook dinner so she could keep sitting in it. Choose from caramel or warm taupe colorways—or maybe get both, one for the living room and one for the den.

You won’t regret investing in the Article Cigar leather sofa, which has the “soft buttery” rawhide tan texture of a baseball glove, but the rounded curves of a marshmallow. “The Cigar sofa’s back is on the lower side, but I cannot emphasize enough how perfect it is for a New York City apartment with a doorway so narrow it is functionally a nightclub bouncer for furniture,” says digital features editor Charlotte Collins. She noted in her review that the substantial seat padding doesn’t leave any indents and that the silhouette is deep enough to comfortably enjoy a nap. She compares it to “an heirloom piece repurposed for modern interiors.”

The seat on this DWR sofa is leveled up by aluminum legs, so it doesn’t look bulky in the way other grounded designs do. We appreciate the modern details such as the minimal lines and cubic silhouette that inspired Torbjørn Anderssen and Espen Voll, the Nordic designers who created the Outline sofa exclusively for Muuto. It comes in as many as 25 different leather variations that include neutrals like the more traditional cognac to the more design-forward mink and birch. For a smaller footprint, opt for the 67-inch-wide loveseat.

Slim, stilted iron legs give this sofa some midcentury flair—a sound choice of silhouette if you want something trend-resistant that can adapt from one home to the next. Refined details like double-stitched seams and aniline-dyed leather give it staying power. And while a lower back makes it less appealing as a movie night staple, it’s deep enough for occasional snoozing, or serving your reading sessions very well.

Low-slung and clean-lined with a platform base, the Bauhaus-influenced Henley sofa would fit well in a stylish penthouse or add flair to a modern living room flush with chrome accents. The mixed-material sofa’s supple leather softens its industrial-looking stainless-steel foundation. Its tufted backrest brings a sophistication to the design, and slimmer armrests add polish to the overall silhouette.

Not only do AD editors love Quince bedding, but we also have a sweet spot for this Italian leather couch. The low-profile design is classic but not boring, and able to evolve with your design tastes. With two comfy chaises, the design is especially well-suited for spaces where lounging or a conversation pit–style seating is the main goal. Atop the sturdy wooden frame sit layers of plush, high-density foam and removable cushions. Solid wood block legs just peeking out from below complete the look.

This reclining sofa from Crate & Barrel’s Drift line has won over many satisfied reviewers who appreciate that the deep-seated model has a contemporary look and a generous amount of cushioning. Design-wise, the Drift Power Triple is clean-lined, with wide arms, waterfall curves, and camouflaged recliner mechanisms that keep the overall appearance streamlined. The couch’s bold proportions make it a worthy statement piece in your living room or screening space, with especially inviting elements for Netflix binge-a-thons.

Some pieces of furniture just look like they’ll age well, and such is the case with this tufted design from CB2’s Gianfranco Frattini collaboration. An influential architect and designer who helped shape the 1950s and 1960s Italian design movement, Frattini’s ethos is apparent in this sofa, which reimagines traditional elements like tufting for modern tastes. In an unexpected hue like grey, the sofa has the potential to bring richly layered Italian design to any living room.

This classic couch is available in forty different genuine and faux leather options if you’ve been having trouble finding the perfect shade of olive green or a chestnut without red undertones. If that’s too much decision fatigue, we recommend going with a versatile shade of black, mostly because the slim arms bear a resemblance to the luxuriously lived-in black leather sofa we spotted in Walton Goggins’ upstate New York retreat.

Cushy doesn’t even begin to describe the Grantham sofa, whose substantial silhouette can easily take on the load of grounding your entire living room’s design scheme. If lounging is your main goal, extra padding on the already oversized arms eliminates the need for spare throw pillows, while the equally cushioned backrest makes it inviting for future movie nights.

The silhouette of this piece, a collaboration between furniture brand Four Hands and Los Angeles–based interior designer Amber Lewis, feels like an updated take on your parents’ bulky Chesterfield sofa. The built-in cushioning helps give the sofa a more elongated look and, while it could feel too formal for casual spaces, the front legs are fitted with tiny, charming brass casters that bring some unexpected levity to the silhouette.

While a lot of leather sofas look like clones of one another, this three-seater from Soho Home stands out among the pack thanks to its handsome wood frame and casually elevated feel. A shade like chestnut or sangria blend in more seamlessly with the oak wood for a more understated look, while the moss, honey, or classic black hues accentuate the soft edge frame to create a statement piece for your living room.

Maiden Home’s modular Jones Sofa has versatility on its side. Not only is it available in various leather styles and a range of hues, but its pieces are detachable so you can rearrange them to align with your seating needs. Not feeling a three-seater anymore? Convert the set into a loveseat and an armless chair. The low slung sofa draws its design from 1970s influences, with oversized cushions and plush arms. Tailored flange seams add structure to the seams, achieving sophistication without lacking comfort.

Personality is one thing that this vintage de Sede leather couch has no shortage of. Just looking at it makes us want to drape ourselves over the U-shaped frame. Manufactured in Switzerland, the style is all about capturing mid-20th-century craftsmanship that lends itself to comfort. Retro vibes are not to be overlooked.

In a sea of black, brown, and tan leather sofas, Sixpenny’s curved Camino provides you with an opportunity to branch out with its playful “North Atlantic” shade of blue. At the same time, the durable, top-hide nubuck leather is well-suited to handle long naps and family movie nights.

For one, leather couches are durable as hell. Leather is said to last four times longer than a fabric sofa, not to mention the patina of aged leather is currently in vogue. It’s worth it: Durable leather furniture is going to stick with you for the long run. Plus, leather is hypoallergenic. Whatever type of leather you choose, no open fibers means no catching and holding dust or other allergens. (Take that, pet hair!)

If properly cared for, your leather sofa can last a good 15 to 25 years (or even longer)—just look at all the high-quality de Sede designs, Bellini configurations, and Togos on the vintage resale market. Bonded and faux leather sofas have a shorter lifespan (roughly three to seven years). Read on for more tips and pointers on proper leather care.

Dust your leather couch with a dry cloth regularly. You’ll also want to clean any spills or messes up immediately to prevent liquids or oils from sinking into the material. Just like our own skin, you should keep leather moisturized to maintain its vitalized appearance. Invest in a nice leather conditioner and give your furniture a good rub down once or twice a year to maintain its suppleness and prevent cracks, stains, and any other imperfections from worsening. For darker colored hides, keeping your sofa out of direct sunlight will also help resist fading. Read our explainer on how to clean leather furniture if you want to learn more ways to maintain your shiny new couch.

Believe it or not, there are many categories of leather. Full grain describes the most authentic version of the material—only the animal hair has been removed from the hide and has been naturally dyed. This type of leather is a bit tough at first, but it wears into a deliciously soft material after time. Top-grain leather is a popular option, as it’s been buffed to have a softer look (and feel) from the first sit. Split grain is a less expensive construction, as the first layer of leather has been removed. Bonded leather is another one you’ll see out there; it’s made from scraps of other projects and connected with an adhesive. No matter which one you choose, you’ll get that leather look and smell—it’s a great material for all budgets.

The world of leather sofas is expansive. With most furniture, we find successful decorating is less about the actual material and more about the silhouettes, which is certainly still the case here. Beloved for its versatility and durability, leather can evoke moodier interior vibes or more streamlined setups. Darker hues like black, chocolate brown, or navy blue signal the former—our forever muse Lenny Kravitz knows this better than anyone. Lighter tans, beiges, and taupes can feel cozy, like the vintage-looking couch we saw in this Brooklyn apartment with lots of sunlight. Since it’s one of the most popular upholsteries for modern furniture, the list of available sofa styles is endless: From classic midcentury-modern shapes with vintage appeal to softer, low profile designs that can outfit modern spaces like Devin Booker’s living room. In brighter hues, leather sofas can even be true statement pieces, like the textural scene-stealer in this Parisian apartment.

The primary suite boasts yet another fireplace, a wet bar, a bathroom, two spacious dressing rooms, and walls that slide open to a sizable balcony. Outdoor amenities include a pool and spa, a kitchen, a putting green, a projection screen, and a fire pit. A guesthouse with a billiards pub and a movie theater round out the compound. #4995. Musk declares Tesla Robotaxi win over Waymo, but Waymo still drives 7x more

Elon Musk says Tesla’s Robotaxi race against Waymo “was never a real contest” after Tesla’s driverless fleet logged 438,000 miles in Austin in a single week, edging past Waymo’s 406,000 weekly average in the city.

That’s one city, one week, and a Waymo figure that’s four months old. Across the US, Waymo still drives about seven times more driverless miles than Tesla every week.

The chart going around comes from Robotaxi Tracker, a community project that pulls driverless mileage from each company’s own reports: Tesla’s weekly Robotaxi safety report and Waymo’s quarterly Safety Impact data hub.

Jason Scharf posted the Austin view on X with the comment that “the battle for Autonomous Austin might already be over.” Musk replied overnight: “It was never a real contest.”

The Austin data is real –albeit with some caveats. In the week ending October 18, Tesla reported 438,000 miles with no safety monitor aboard in Austin, up 30% from the week before. Waymo averaged about 406,000 miles a week in Austin in the second quarter.

But the tracker’s own caption explains the catch. Tesla’s figure is its latest week. Waymo’s is an average of April through June, because Waymo publishes about three months after each quarter ends. So Musk is comparing Tesla’s best week ever to Waymo’s spring.

Switch the same chart to “All markets” and the story flips.

Waymo averaged 3.90 million rider-only miles per week in Q2 2026. Tesla did 558,000 miles in its best week. Waymo’s Phoenix, Bay Area, and Los Angeles markets each drive more miles per week on their own than Tesla’s entire network.

Cumulative numbers are even more lopsided. Waymo has logged 271.3 million rider-only miles since 2020, including 50.7 million in Q2 alone, as we covered when Waymo released its latest safety data. Tesla has 3.11 million driverless miles since January 22, 2026. Waymo drove more than that in a single week.

And Tesla’s total leans almost entirely on one city:

Austin accounts for 79% of Tesla’s weekly driverless miles. Houston, Tampa, Orlando, and Miami combined did about 25,000 miles. Waymo’s smallest tracked market, Atlanta, averaged 250,000 a week in Q2.

The Austin surge has an obvious cause. Tesla launched public Cybercab rides in Austin on September 3.

According to Robotaxi Tracker’s count of the Texas DMV automated-vehicle list, Tesla now has 463 Cybercabs registered in Texas, 337 of them added in the last 30 days. That’s 48% of the 970 autonomous vehicles Tesla has registered in the state.

Tesla isn’t hiding the strategy. On the Q3 earnings call last week, Ashok Elluswamy, Tesla’s head of AI software, said the company wants to “swarm Austin with Cybercabs everywhere.” Musk said the Austin Cybercab fleet had grown “by a factor of 10” in about a month.

So Tesla dumped hundreds of new, company-owned cars into one metro and the miles went up. That’s fleet deployment. It doesn’t show the software can do what Waymo does in 15 cities, and Tesla can’t repeat it everywhere at once.

The bigger problem is what the cars are allowed to do.

On the same earnings call, Elluswamy described the service as constrained by the software, not by regulators. He said that if there’s “the slightest possible risk at all,” Tesla doesn’t take it on, and that the limits are “technology driven” for now. Service hours only recently stretched from 9 p.m. toward 2 a.m., and he listed highways, “higher speed roads,” 24/7 operation, and more weather conditions as things Tesla still has to unlock.

You can see those limits in the rides. Tesla Robotaxis don’t take highways, and riders report they avoid at-grade railroad crossings. In September, a rider had a 10-minute Cybercab trip in Austin turn into a 70-minute detour because the car drove south through East Austin until it found a bridge over the rail line, instead of taking MoPac.

That matters for this comparison, too. Miles is a flattering metric when your car takes the long way. A trip that should cover a few miles and instead loops across town logs a lot more mileage for the same rider.

Waymo, meanwhile, has been taking riders on freeways in Phoenix, San Francisco, and Los Angeles since November 2025.

Credit where it’s due: Tesla’s driverless miles are growing fast, 30% in a single week, and Austin now has a real Tesla fleet carrying the public without anyone in the driver’s seat. A year ago, that wasn’t the case.

I continue to hear tesla refer to its miles as 'driverless miles', but Waymo's as rider only miles. This can be a big difference. Are you sure that this is not an apples and oranges comparison? Do we have confidence that Tesla is only including miles with a rider inside and not driverless miles where the can is spinning circles in a parking lot??

But “never a real contest” is a strange thing to say when you’re behind by 7x on weekly miles and 87x on cumulative miles, and your one win depends on comparing your newest week to your rival’s data from last spring.

What stands out to us is the type of autonomy Tesla is deploying. In 2020, Musk wrote that “Waymo is impressive, but a highly specialized solution,” and that Tesla’s approach was “a general solution.” For years, Tesla fans dismissed Waymo as a geofenced system that only worked in mapped areas with lots of restrictions.

Now look at Tesla’s Robotaxi: geofenced, no highways, avoiding railroad crossings, taking 70 minutes for 10-minute trips, and limited by hours because the software isn’t ready for anything with “the slightest possible risk.” By Musk’s own old definition, that’s a highly specialized solution. Waymo is the one driving on freeways now.

Robotaxis still need electricity, and so does your EV. If you drive electric, pairing it with home solar can cut your fuel costs to nearly zero. #4993. Coinbase’s US national trust bank charter takes effect

Coinbase plans to expand its institutional crypto custody business under the federal charter and is exploring digital asset payment products.

Coinbase’s national trust bank charter took effect on Friday, according to the US Office of the Comptroller of the Currency (OCC).

The applied for the charter in October 2025 and received preliminary approval in April 2026, according to the OCC’s filing record. The charter allows Coinbase National Trust Company to hold digital assets on behalf of clients as a fiduciary, according to the OCC’s preliminary approval letter.

Coinbase plans to offer institutional clients segregated custody accounts, with access to staking, financing and trading through other Coinbase entities, according to its charter application.

Coinbase’s application also outlined plans to explore digital asset payment products and custody services that would allow clients to interact with select smart contracts.

Other crypto companies that received OCC national trust bank charters include Ripple, Circle, BitGo, Fidelity Digital Assets and Paxos.

Crypto.com and Stripe-owned Bridge received conditional approval, while Kraken and EDX Markets are still pursuing charters.

#4992. The 2027 Kia EV9 costs $90 more, but at least you get a few extras

Kia barely touched the 2027 EV9’s price, raising it by only $90 while sneaking in a few extra standard features on select trims.

Kia’s three-row electric SUV returns for the 2027 model year, mostly as a carryover model with a few design and packaging updates.

The 2027 Kia EV9 starts at $54,990, up a measly $90 from last year’s model. That’s for the base EV9 Light short-range trim, which uses a 76.1 kWh battery and delivers up to 230 miles of range.

Higher-priced trims, including the Light Long Range, Wind, Land, and GT-Line, use a larger 99.8 kWh battery that delivers up to 305 miles of range in RWD variants.

The Wind and Land trims have standard all-wheel-drive (AWD) and offer up to 283 miles of range, while the GT-Line is also AWD but offers slightly less range at 280 miles.

Kia’s dual-motor e-AWD system delivers up to 379 hp and 516 lb-ft of torque in the EV9 GT-Line, enabling it to accelerate from 0 to 60 mph in 4.5 seconds.

For the 2027 model, the EV9 Wind now comes with black-finish 19″ wheels and the Glossy Black exterior paint color as standard, and Wolf Gray paint is newly available as an option.

The 2027 EV9 Land now includes the Nightfall Edition Package, which was previously optional, as standard. It starts at $69,990, or $500 less than the 2026 Kia EV9 Land Nightfall Edition.

The package includes a considerable performance boost with torque rising from 379 lb-ft to 516 lb-ft, plus an exclusive black interior and exterior design elements.

Inside, the 2027 EV9 features Kia’s Digital Cockpit with nearly 30″ of combined displays. The setup includes dual 12.3″ instrument displays and navigation screens in a curved panoramic display, plus a 5″ climate control screen.

Kia’s three-row electric SUV offers up to 42.8″ of legroom in the second row, which is even more than the 2025 Cadillac Escalade. It’s available in six- and seven-seat configurations.

The EV9 is 197.2″ long, 77.9″ wide, and 68.9″ tall, with a 122″ wheelbase, or slightly smaller than the Rivian R1S and Hyundai IONIQ 9.

With DC fast charging of up to 350 kW, the 2027 Kia EV9’s battery can charge from 10% to 80% in as little as 24 minutes.

All 2027 Kia EV9s also come with a built-in NACS charge port, so you can use Tesla Superchargers without a separate adaptor.

#4991. Billy Joel at Home: 10 Photos of the Piano Man Through the Decades

For Billy Joel, New York is more than just a place. It’s an inextricable part of what makes the singer who he is. “My speech patterns are New York. The music that I like, it was all New York–based. That’s what I think of when I think of home,” he said in his 2025 HBO documentary Billy Joel: And So It Goes. “It’s my roots.”

The Bronx native grew up in Hicksville, a middle-class suburb of Nassau County on Long Island. His family struggled to make ends meet. “There was poor, and then there was Billy Joel poor. He was lucky if he had enough money to buy a pack of cigarettes,” his childhood friend and fellow musician Jon Small said in And So It Goes. According to Joel’s sister Judy, no matter how hard times were, their mother Rosalind insisted he take piano lessons. His talent “had to be nurtured,” Judy said in the HBO doc. “She knew that he was going to be something someday.”

Before he indeed became something, Joel worked on an oyster boat. “I used to look up at this mansion on the hill and wonder what it would be like to live in a house like that,” he said in the documentary, referencing the waterfront property on Centre Island he nicknamed MiddleSea. “I used to think, ‘They’re rich bastards. They never had to work a day in their life.’ Well, I own that house now. It’s not finished yet. And neither am I.”

Read on for a glimpse into the Grammy winner’s life, from his early days in New York to his brief stint in Los Angeles and return home.

Joel’s music career took the Long Island native to Los Angeles in the early 1970s, and he signed with Columbia Records in 1973. After three years in LA, he and his first wife, Elizabeth Weber, returned to his home state, where he completed his fourth studio album, Turnstiles. “I wanted to get back to New York and make more of a rock and roll album,” Joel explained in And So It Goes. “I was on a bus on my way back to Highland Falls, New York. Elizabeth found us a house up on the Hudson River, and it was fall. It was just gorgeous. The colors were ablaze, the trees were, like, gold and red, and I remember thinking, ‘This is the New York State that I loved when I was a kid. This is magic. We don’t have this in California.’ And I wrote this love song to New York on the bus.” It would become “New York State of Mind,” one of Joel’s most popular tunes.

The singer-songwriter poses with some of his records in January 1978.

Joel’s fifth studio album, 1977’s The Stranger, was a runaway success—but the singer, pictured alongside his accolades in New York in 1978, told Playboy a few years later that his newfound fortune didn’t faze him. “I read these stories about Peter Frampton’s multimillion-dollar mansion, 15 Rolls-Royces and all that,” he said. “Who cares? It’s all bullshit. What was his last record like? Was it any good? That’s the question. People get hung up on the money thing. It’s nice to have four motorcycles. It’s fun. And I walk into the garage and go, ‘Holy shit! I’ve got four motorcycles!’ But I don’t go beyond a certain point.”

Joel, his then-wife Elizabeth, and their dog in 1978.

Instead of splurging on a million-dollar mansion, Joel told Playboy that when he and his wife decided to buy a home, “We got a $300,000 house with a mortgage like everybody else.” Their glass house, featuring floor-to-ceiling windows overlooking the Long Island Sound, was located in the town of Oyster Bay on Long Island’s North Shore. Joel appears poised to hurl a rock at the home on the cover of his 1980 album, Glass Houses. “The old thing about people who live in glass houses shouldn’t throw stones—I don’t believe it,” he said. “I think, Why not? Take chances. I really wanted to throw a rock at my own house.”

Joel and Elizabeth at their Long Island residence.

In the 1970s, Joel asked Weber to be his manager. “The company I’m with now is New York–based,” Joel said of his representation. “It’s called Home Run—nothing to do with baseball. It’s a home-run agency.” Weber got right to work. She recalled in his HBO documentary that the “story [Joel] likes to tell” of her early days on the job is that he woke up the next morning to phones everywhere and “cabinets installed.” However, she said, “That’s not really true, but it’s close to true.” The couple (seen here at Joel’s grand piano in their living room) split time between their Manhattan apartment, where they worked, and Oyster Bay. Weber left her role managing Joel when the two split, and her brother Frank picked up where she left off. Home Run dissolved amid financial woes in the ’80s, and Joel eventually pressed charges against Frank over mishandled funds; the parties reached a settlement, the terms of which have not been disclosed publicly.

The musician mixing tapes in the music studio of his New York home.

The Oyster Bay house, a midcentury-modern residence, was not far from where Joel grew up in Hicksville. Photographed here in his at-home music studio in 1978, the singer’s career was on the upswing—but his relationship with Weber was growing tense. “Having a big boom in record success, I think my marriage was starting to unravel around then; 1979, it wasn’t the happiest time,” Joel once said. “Record success doesn’t translate into happiness.” Joel assumed things were paying off financially, but he admitted he “wasn’t aware” how much money he was actually bringing in. “I know we were living in a nice house, my wife was going out and buying all kinds of nice stuff,” he said. “I never really went out and did that.”

Joel multitasks in his LA home studio in 1984.

Though Joel had settled down on the East Coast, he told the Los Angeles Times that his California years cross his mind “all the time.” The singer, pictured here in LA in 1984, wrote his hit song “Piano Man” based on his experience as a lounge musician (using the name Bill Martin) at a now-defunct bar near the corner of Wilshire Boulevard and Western Avenue during the early 1970s. “I wonder what happened to the people I knew—the people in the bar in ‘Piano Man,’” he said. “It was the Executive Room in the Wilshire district. I got tips and made union scale, and I was able to afford an apartment in Studio City. The weather was great; the people were nice.”

Though it appears Joel lived like a rock star in Los Angeles—as seen here in 1984, perched on a sheepskin-style throw over a leather sofa with a pack of Marlboros and a landline telephone by his side—he told the Los Angeles Times in 2023 that he felt more like “an exiled writer living in Paris” during his early stint in the City of Angels. “I went from being an insignificant nobody to the Piano Man while I was there,” he said. “I still look back to my time in LA with a certain amount of romance.”

Joel is pictured here at the dinner table in 2001. When The New Yorker paid him a visit at his Centre Island residence in 2014, he described the interior’s aesthetic as “Catholic martyrs and saints, grotesqueries.” In the library, Joel—who decorated the place himself—had a model of the Queen Mary on his mantel, and in the living room facing the fireplace was a triptych of the Last Supper along with silver jugs and bowls on a nearby table.

Joel’s various properties have passed through some famous hands. He and his second wife, supermodel Christie Brinkley, owned a 12-acre oceanfront property in the East Hampton hamlet of Amagansett. The couple raised daughter Alexa Ray Joel in the Long Island dwelling. It was later sold to Jerry and Jessica Seinfeld for $32 million. According to The New Yorker, Joel also sold a duplex on Central Park West to singer-songwriter Sting for $5 million and a house on Martha’s Vineyard to businessman Mickey Drexler. At one point, Joel purchased a home in the Hamptons from Jaws star Roy Scheider.

“As a little kid, I always liked boats and the romance of living on an island,” Joel told Parade in 2018. The singer—pictured here on an East Hampton dock in 2001—has always felt at home on the water. In 2007 he designed a 57-foot powerboat called the Vendetta that can go about 50 knots, per The New Yorker. Boating represented “freedom,” Joel said in his documentary. “I am the captain of my ship.”

#4988. Korean police book 80 Tesla drivers over illegal FSD unlock devices

Seoul police have booked 80 people for using aftermarket devices to switch on Tesla’s “Full Self-Driving” in cars that aren’t allowed to run it in South Korea. Officers also seized 21 of the devices.

The Seoul Metropolitan Police Agency announced the case on Tuesday. It’s the largest enforcement action yet against the FSD “jailbreak” hardware that has spread through China, Europe, and Korea over the past year.

Police say the suspects either imported the unlock modules from Europe and China or made their own. One plugged a European-made unit into his own Tesla. Another, an IT worker, built his device with information he found on GitHub.

Suspects also swapped advice online on how to unlock FSD and how to stop the car from keeping any record that it had been activated.

They’re charged under Korea’s Motor Vehicle Management Act, which bans unauthorized software changes that can affect a vehicle’s safety. The penalty is up to two years in prison or a fine of up to 20 million won (about $15,000).

The Ministry of Land, Infrastructure and Transport asked police to look into it back in April after finding signs of unauthorized activation. A police official told Korea JoongAng Daily that “safety risks were confirmed,” including the possibility of FSD misreading driving conditions. The investigation is ongoing, with police consulting the ministry and the Korea Transportation Safety Authority on the technical side.

Tesla launched FSD (Supervised) in South Korea in late 2025, but only on US-built vehicles: Model S, Model X, and Cybertruck. Those cars are exempt from Korean safety certification under the Korea-US free trade agreement. The Model 3 and Model Y, which Tesla ships to Korea from Giga Shanghai, haven’t cleared Korea’s safety standards for the feature.

And those two models are the bulk of the fleet. Citing Carisyou Data Lab, Korea JoongAng Daily reports that 47,458 of the 229,311 Teslas on Korean roads as of August, or 20.7%, can legally use FSD.

That number doesn’t match the government’s own count from a few months earlier. In May, data released by lawmaker Park Yong-gap showed just 4,292 of 180,684 registered Teslas (2.4%) were eligible: 2,708 Model X, 1,193 Model S, and 391 Cybertruck. Either way, the vast majority of Korean Tesla owners can’t legally use FSD, and some of them paid over $6,000 for it anyway.

The hardware is the same, which is why the hack works. The modules plug into the car’s CAN bus and get around the regional software lock.

Tesla has been fighting the devices itself. In April, it started remotely detecting them and stripping FSD from affected cars, warned owners they’d be fully liable for any crash and could lose warranty coverage, and permanently banned some Chinese owners from FSD.

Tesla & Starlink: The reality as reported in 2021: every one of our Tesla autos are connected 24/7/365 to the company. It was that year that a programming glitch came where some vehicles would not function. That was quickly corrected. In the news today where proof that when Elon shut down Starlink to Ukraine, russian missiles came and killed 5 people, including one baby. ... here is the reality you and me must face: Musk holds the power right now, that if he decides he does not like 'some owners' of Teslas, he can SHUT DOWN instantly those vehicles from moving. It is not enough that potentially, every conversation and communication device used inside those vehicles 'can' be recorded but, as he has proven perfectly willing to do to Ukraine families, so he could at any moment 'shut down' some specific users of Tesla vehicles at-will. this is not hype, fantasy or gusswork. This is how the online active update software communications system works on every car we buy from Tesla. My company is looking into other providers of Sat-Com services, and we are re-evaluating our own vehicle choices.

It’s easy to see why people do this. Picture a Korean owner of a Shanghai-built Model Y who paid for FSD, watching the Model X next door run the same software on the same computer. That’s frustrating.

Tesla sold FSD to owners of cars it had no regulatory path to enable in Korea, the same way it sold “Full Self-Driving” in Europe for years while approval kept slipping. It only dropped the name there this month after Germany pushed back. Sell a feature people can’t legally use and you create the market for the jailbreak.

Korea has now shown it will actually prosecute. So will Tesla refund the Korean owners who bought a feature their cars may never be allowed to run?

#4987. Sykeruns X7 electric dirt bike review: A lot of power for surprisingly little money

The mini electric dirt bike market has exploded over the last few years, with seemingly countless new brands offering everything from pint-sized electric pit bikes to surprisingly powerful off-road machines. The Sykeruns X7 falls somewhere in the middle, offering enough power to get into some serious trouble while keeping the price relatively approachable.

I’ve spent some time now riding the X7, and while it certainly isn’t the most refined electric dirt bike I’ve tested, there’s enough to like about what you’re getting for the money.

In fact, if you’re looking for a reasonably powerful electric dirt bike for recreational trail riding without spending several thousand dollars, this might be worth a closer look.

The Sykeruns X7 is a compact electric dirt bike featuring a 5,500W peak-rated motor, which translates to around 7.4 horsepower. That’s quite a bit of power for a bike at this price point of $1,699, especially considering that many similarly priced electric two-wheelers are significantly less powerful. Oh, and for our readers, we’ve gotten you set up with a 20% discount. Just use code ELECTREK20 to drop the price down to an impressive $1,359!

Sykeruns claims a top speed of 57 mph (92 km/h), though since this is an off-road only bike, you’ll rarely get the chance to put that claim to the test. I certainly didn’t, as I didn’t have a long enough and flat enough stretch of trail where I felt comfortable hitting nearly highway speeds on a low-cost e-moto.

The battery is a 60V 27Ah pack, providing 1,620 Wh of capacity. Sykeruns claims up to 60 miles (96 km) of range, though as with virtually every electric dirt bike, your actual range will depend heavily on how aggressively you’re riding. Fast trail riding, steep climbs, and repeated hard acceleration can eat through battery capacity much more quickly than leisurely cruising. Suffice it to say that you’ll likely not see the 60 mile figure in real world scenarios, but that’s still a fairly large battery.

The bike rolls on a 17-inch front wheel and 14-inch rear wheel, both wrapped in knobby off-road tires. Hydraulic disc brakes handle stopping duties, while an inverted suspension fork and rear monoshock help smooth out the bumps.

At 143 lb (65 kg), the X7 isn’t exactly featherweight, but it’s also far from being a full-size electric motorcycle.

The first thing I was surprised about with the X7 is that it offers a pretty decent amount of performance for the money. There’s enough power here to have some real fun, and the bike doesn’t feel like one of those underpowered mini electric motorcycles that struggles once you get beyond casual neighborhood speeds.

That said, I wouldn’t describe the acceleration as mind-blowing, either. We’re not talking about the kind of explosive power delivery you’d get from a high-end electric dirt bike costing several times as much. Instead, the X7 delivers a healthy amount of acceleration that feels appropriate for a recreational off-road machine.

And let’s be real here, that’s probably what most people shopping in this budget category are looking for.

The suspension is another area where the X7 earns a passing grade without necessarily winning any awards. The front fork and rear shock do their jobs, absorbing the bumps and ruts you’d expect to encounter on typical trails. It’s nothing I’d call plush, but I could see it doing light jumps with minimal air time.

Would I want to take this thing to a motocross track and start launching it off massive jumps? Not particularly. The suspension doesn’t have the premium feel or sophisticated damping of more expensive machines.

But for general trail riding, exploring dirt roads, and the occasional overlanding shenanigans, it’s perfectly serviceable. Not every electric dirt bike needs to be a competition-ready machine, and the X7 seems comfortable occupying that more recreational category.

I was also pleasantly surprised by the tires, which seem well suited to the bike’s intended purpose. The knobby tread offers a reasonable amount of grip for off-road riding, and I didn’t find myself immediately wishing for a tire upgrade.

The overall size is worth mentioning, too. This isn’t a particularly large electric dirt bike, but at my modest 5’7” (170 cm) height, I felt comfortable on it. The riding position didn’t feel overly cramped, and the bike was manageable enough that I didn’t feel like I was wrestling with an oversized machine.

Taller riders might find it a bit more compact than they’d prefer, but for average-height riders, the proportions seem reasonable.

If there’s one area where the X7 reminds you of its budget-friendly $1,699 price tag, it’s the overall level of refinement.

Nothing about the bike struck me as particularly premium. The components, finishes, and general construction are more in line with what I’d expect from a relatively inexpensive electric dirt bike.

But that’s not necessarily a criticism. There’s a difference between something feeling inexpensive and something feeling inadequate, and the X7 generally stays on the acceptable side of that line.

The controls are straightforward, the digital display provides the basic information you need, and the overall package feels focused on delivering usable performance rather than impressing riders with fancy features.

The grippy hydraulic brakes are also a welcome inclusion, especially considering the speeds this bike is capable of reaching. With a machine that can approach highway speeds, having proper motorcycle-style braking hardware is important.

Of course, long-term durability is a separate question. Budget electric dirt bikes don’t always have the same track record, parts availability, or established dealer networks as the more recognizable motorcycle brands. That’s something worth considering before purchasing, particularly if you plan to ride hard or frequently.

One thing I appreciate about the Sykeruns X7 is that the company makes its intended use fairly clear.

There’s a large red warning sticker on the bike explaining that it’s intended for off-road use only. That’s a good thing, especially in today’s market, where the lines between electric bicycles, electric dirt bikes, and electric motorcycles are becoming increasingly blurred, despite laws existing to keep these types of bikes out of the bike lane and off of public roads.

The X7 is absolutely not an electric bicycle. It doesn’t have pedals, it produces several thousand watts of power, and it’s capable of speeds far beyond anything permitted under electric bicycle regulations.

It’s an off-road electric motorcycle, plain and simple.

Interestingly, though, Sykeruns has equipped the bike with features like a tail light and turn signals, which could potentially give some riders the wrong impression.

Those are certainly useful features on a motorcycle, but they don’t magically make a vehicle street legal. Without the necessary certification, registration, and other road-use requirements, the X7 remains an off-road-only machine.

I worry that some buyers might see those lights and assume they can ride the bike around town like a small electric motorcycle. That’s not the case, and it’s important to understand that distinction before buying one.

After spending time with the Sykeruns X7, my biggest takeaway is that this is a reasonably capable electric dirt bike at a pretty compelling price.

It’s not the best electric dirt bike I’ve ever ridden. The suspension is fairly basic, the build quality is unremarkable, and there’s nothing particularly groundbreaking about the design.

But for $1,699 (or $1,359 if you use the code ELECTREK20 to take 20% off!), you’re getting a 5.5 kW peak-rated electric motorcycle with a sizable battery, proper off-road tires, hydraulic brakes, and enough performance to have some genuine fun on the trails.

I wouldn’t say that the X7 here is trying to compete with more up-market electric dirt bikes from the likes of Juiced, Sur-Ron or Talaria on overall refinement, but they make this type of riding much more accessible.

And that’s really where I think the X7 makes the most sense.

If you’re an experienced dirt bike rider looking for a highly refined machine with excellent suspension and premium components, you’ll probably want to keep shopping.

But if you’re someone who wants a fun electric dirt bike for exploring private property, cruising around trails where motorized riding is permitted, or just enjoying some recreational off-road riding without spending a fortune, the Sykeruns X7 offers a pretty reasonable package.

It doesn’t need to be amazing at everything. At this price, being pretty good at most things is already an accomplishment.
